[ALL RIGLITS RESERVED.]

TWO PARTAFIS

A TALE, Br

FERDINAND VON SAAR.

PART IL.

When Tertschka and George reached the cottage, the others were crowding round the fire, eating out of half-broken basins. The foreman was standing near, his sleeves turned up and an apron tied round his waist. He was just beginning to carve a huge piece of meat, the smell of which was tickling the appetites of those who had entered. George heaved an involuntary sigh. The others looked eagerly at the meat. One after another they went for their portions, which they then proceeded to devour without the ceremony of knife or fork. Some of the men laid down money for the meat, but in most cases the foreman made a sign in a little book. George, having got a basin from Tertschka, now approached the fire with it. The foreman looked at him in surprise. At last he seemed to remember him.

"Aha, the manikin of yesterday. Well, what have you been doing?"

" greaking Abonee-." "And now you want to eat—and what do you want to eat ?"

"Give me some gruel and potatoes, please." The foreman put into George's basin what he asked for, and took the coin which was tendered. "A slice of meat, too, I warrant ? " he then added. This was a trying temptation indeed to the poor fellow. But, remembering Tertschka's advice, he replied, just as the foreman was beginning to carve, "No, I don't want any meat." , "What, are you a miser 1 You look half starved; you should want to get something sub- stantial into your stomach." "He has the fever, the fat of the meat would harm him," interposed Terts:dika, stepping forward. She felt it necessary to support George against the foreman's threatened brutality. "Hold your tongue," shouted he. "How do you know what will harm him and what not? Don't interfere with what doesn't concern you." Then, turning to George: "Well, do you or do you not want any meat?" These words scunded like a command not to refuse the tempting dish. Shy as he was, George gathered up all his courage and said: "She's right, I ought not to eat any." " Very well," the foreman cried, angrily, throwing down his knife, "I shan't beg of you, that's certain." And as George didn't move away, he asked, "What are you waiting for ? " "For the change." "You, perhaps, thought I meant to keep those shabby pennies of yours," said the foreman, as he contemptuously flung over the coins and turned away. George, the basin in one hand, picked the money up with the other, and sitting down in a remote corner, ate up his scanty meal, already half cold with the delay. Occupied thus, he saw the- fore- Man bring out a greenish sort of flask and pour brandy out of it into a little glass for those who asked for it. Emptied and then refilled, it made its round from mouth to mouth, but remembering Tertschka's advice he looked forward to the evening for that good fare. Tertschka had been making a rather scanty dinner, and at her stepfather's command was now preparing to clean up. The others went yawning to the shady side of the cottage to sleep through the hour of rest. The foreman snatched from the fire a little frying pan, on which was a roasted chicken, and together with a bottle of wine he placed it on a little table -standing near. While preparing comfortably to set about his meal, his eye chanced to fall upon George, who, with his dish on his knees, was considering whether he ought not to help Tertschka to clean the dishes. A vague fear of the brutal man held kim back, however, from doing it. "Why are you sitting there, staring. Get away to the rest. I don't want a spy here, to frighten away the food from my mouth;" George, startled out of his meditation, stole away to stretch himself down on the Sunny ground, as there was no room left in the shade. Soon after the foreman's bell rang again to summon the labourers back to their work'. He himself retired to his room, there to indulge in a sleep. Giving a final stretch to their limbs, the men got up from the ground, reluctantly obeying the summons. Some of them, however, merely turned over and slept on. George and Tertschka went together down to the stone pit and worked hard till night came on. The following days were spent much in the same way. George's strength was rising. The bitterest of misery was past, and the vivifying air of the mountains seemed to operate on his frame. He swung his hammer stoutly, telling his poor com- panion all kinds of things out of his soldier's life. True, these neither comprised adventurous frolics nor deeds of daring soldiership. But, considering the shyness of his disposition, and cast, as he always was, upon his own resources, he could not possibly have become acquainted with the sunny side of his profession, which to many a one reveals all the pleasantness of existence. He was only able to tell of his sufferings during the years he was a recruit, which the relentless treatment of his sergeant had made a very hell to him, of endless sentry-standing in the snow, difficult marches, nightly campaigns in rain and storm, and, above all, of the Siege of Venice. Camping before the fortress of Malgera with his regiment, hundreds and hundreds, he assured her, had been snatched off in the loathsome air of the marshes, by typhus fever or cholera.

Tertschka listened in silence. Many things she did not comprehend at all, others only partially. What he told was so strange, so remote from the things she knew of. She could not fancy a city erected in the midst of the waters. The word "sea" implied to her imagination nothing more than a shining and indistinct little cloud. But she guessed from all this that George had gone through misery all his life long. She in her turn told him -what sad recollections she possessed of her mono- tonous and dreary life. So, unconsciously, they found comfort in each other. It was an enjoyment every morning to go down together to where they were working with their hamin era on their shoulders, thus to spend the sunny days in company. It often happened that the sound of the dinner bell ringing broke in upon their melancholy yet dear happiness with a sudden surprise. Down they went to mix with the others in the cottage. Their silent resig- nation and their very misery had united them even more closely than the plentifulness of life and its raptures ever could do. Maybe the foreman had heard of this intimacy through the mischievous reports of their fellow- workmen; or had the evil instincts of his nature made him guess more than the truth? One day he unexpectedly came up behind them. "Why are you cowering here like a couple of toads?" he shouted. They looked up terrified. "Off with you, you wretch, away with you to your comrades—there's your place." And with a gesture of command he pointed in the direction of the stone pit. "And you, you hypocrite," he cried to Tertschka, whilst George, speechless with terror and bewilderment, was obeying his orders, "I really believe you were keeping company with yon miserable cripple ! I'll teach you to stop that off, that's certain. If I find you together again, I'll whip the jackass off for good, and as for yourself, you shan't see daylight any more." Thus, unexpectedly and without pity, were they separated. On the following morning George was down near the railroad doing his work, and when at noon and in the evening they met in the cottage, they didn't dare to look at each other, far less-to exchange a single word. The foreman kept his eyes close upon them, and the others seemed to watch them too with sullen mischievousness. One night, it was Saturday, the foreman, together with -some of his fellow-drinkers, went to a public-ho in a neighbouring town. Those who. remained behind were amusing themselves—as was usually the case after wages had been paid—by playing at cards, and these, which were in a filthy state, were making their rounds amongst them. The scene growing noisy and dissolute, George took courage and stole over to Tertschka. She was sitting on her old chest in the corner where she ordinarily slept, with her head resting in her hand. "Tertschka," he said, in a whisper, pulling a small leather purse out of his pocket, "here's the last of what I owe you," and he carefully com- menced to lay some money in her lap. "Don't," she said, "I'm sure you'll want it some day." "What should I want it for," he replied, des- pondingly, "there is no joy left for me on earth since I can't work with you any longer." "Nor to me, either," she answered, in a whisper. "Why has he separated us, I wonder,..” he said, after a pause. "It would be all the same to him if we were sitting together or not, provided we were doing our duty." She dsopped her eyes. "He's a bad man," she said, at last, "who cannot bear to see others happy. His hawinese consists in taking from

others what is aearest to them. That's also the reason why he won't let me go to church. Aiad I cannot pray unless I am kneeling before an Om'. To be sure he knows no God,. and he-used to scold poor mother, who would not miss her Sunday's mass, to which she always took me along with her. But to-morrow I am detee:%ined to go down to Scholtevrein, let him do *lot he may. I won't become quite such a heathen as these people, who think of nothing but drinking and playing cards." She got up, opened the chest she had been sitting upon and drew out of it a woollen jacket, a cotton frock, and a pair of heavy shoes. George looked attentively at what was going on. "I, too, have not been to church for a long time; how nice it would be to go there with you to-morrow 1" "It would be very nice, but it can't be." "But the foreman might not notice it after all. We would each set out alone, and could meet some- where further down.". She reflected a little. "You are right, that would do. But you must start long before me. There's a secret path leading down into the valley on the left side of the cottage ; a wooden cross lies blow; we might meet tare. But do go now," she insisted, anxiously, "lest the others notice our talking together." He complied with her request. Lying on his hard couch, with the noise and disputes of the players in his ears, he cheerfully thought of the next day's pleasures, and soon fell asleep. On the following morning a limpid sun made the earth sparkle allover with glee. George descended the steep footpath Tertschica had told him of. He was looking out for the wooden cross below in the valley. At last he found it, half rotten, and hidden among the shoots of the' young pine trees. It was early in the morning. He sat down on a moss- grown stone, just like an alLar step, at the foot of the cross. The Sabbath's holy silence was all around him; the very bees about the numberless gentians, whose dark blue calyxes were opening, appeared to refrain from buzzing ; George, too, was silent, breathing in, as it were, the surrounding stillness.- It seemed to him as if he heard a low but solemn ringing of bells. By and bye impatience seized him. .He rose and paced to and fro, now and then plucking the white or yellow flowers which abounded. "I'll give them to Tertschka when she comes," he said to himself, seeing with some surprise the nosegay he had been gathering. He plucked a long tern and fastened it to his cap, where, swinging to and fro, it looked like a feather. At length he perceived the fluttering of a womans' gown on the height above, and before long Tertschka was at his side. "Here lam !" she said, taking breath. "He has for once let me go without saying much." George stood looking at her. She wore no ker- chief to-day; her smooth hair lay simply parted over her forehead • the dark jacket, somewhat too loose, and the light coloured frock, bah suited her very well. "How nice you are 100 g to-day," he said, at last. She blushed, and let her eyes fall. "All these things were my mother's," she said, smoothing her frock. "I seldom wear them, so they keep fresh." "Will you take these flowers? I gathered them for you." He took the nosegay he till now had been half hiding behind his back, and prepared to fasten it to her breast. It was too big, however, so she kept it. in her hand, round the fingers of which she had twisted her chaplet. Then they went on walking at each other's side through green meadows and small fields, the corn in which was cut already, and in stook. At last they reached Scholtevrein. There all was bustle, for the churches were open. The little town was thronged with vehicles and people in Sunday dress. Before the church wooden booths were erected, in which were to be got all sorts of wonderful things, laid out in motley array: kerchiefs, pipes, knives, beads of glass and wax, all kinds of china, ginger- bread, and children's toys. They stopped to admire all this finery. Then George wanted to buy a pipe. He used to smoke when in the army, -but later, when things had gone against him, he had been obliged to give it up. Now, with enough to live on, not indulging in either wine or cards, he thought he could afford to resume the old luxury. He told Tertschka of his intention, which she approved, and whilst he made his purchase, she went slowly on, after having told George, that as the Scholtervrein church was crowded, she would go about a mile and a half to a lonely little church which she already knew, and there await him. It was Our Lady's Guard at the foot of the Souvend- stein.

George broke through a crowd of holiday makers and customers, and bought a nice pipe with coloured tassels on it. His eyes then chanced to lighten on a glittering necklace of yellow golden beads, and he pictured to himself how nice it would look on Tertschka's neck. The price asked for it not being high, he had the irinket wrapped iin paper and put it n his pocket. The few coppers given in change he spent on a piece of gingerbread, after which he slipped quickly. into one of the booths to procure some tobacco, and then hastened off with his treasures to rejoin Tertschka. He showed her his pipe which she liked; he then quickly added, giving her the gingerbread "and that's for you." It was adorned with a picture representing a little heart pierced through by an arrow, the whole framed by a wreath of flowers. With a grateful smile Tertschka silently contem. plated it, sliding it in between the flowers and her chaplet. I have got something more for you," he said, after a pause, slowly drawing out of his pocket the little parcel, as he did so allowing the beads tc glitter through the unfolded paper. She glanced at them.

"Why have you spent so much money on me ? " she said, her face glowing with happy surprise and the purest joy. "There is nothing I would not part with for you," he said, with feeling. "You must put it on at once—it will suit you beautifully." Whilst she tried to put on the necklace, Tert- tichka asked George to hold what she had in hex hand. But the netklace fastening behind, she did not immediately succeed in her efforts. "Let me do it for you," he said, returning hex things to her, and softly lifting up her brown braids he made the ends of the clasp meet. "Done," he said, looking at her with a sem tinizing, contented eye. They afterwards walked on cheerfully as far as the little church, which they law peeping out of the surrounding trees. There were few people about. A sulky-looking old priest had just ascended the altar steps, and was beginning to say mass with an air of perfect indifference. Tertschka knelt down in the last row of pews. She laid down the nosegay and the " heart ' and folded her hands. A strati cte feeling seized upon George as he stood behind his companion in this great vault. A soft light broke in through the high yet narrow windows. He heard the murmuring of the priest, the tolling of the bell, and his heart was quivering with inexpressible emotion. Yet h€ could not pray. He looked fixedly at Tertschka, who knelt before him with bent head, her lips slightly moving. Mass was soon over, the priest gave the benediction, and everyone passed out of the church. Tertschka alone remained. At last she crossed herself, and rising up, walked to the door with George where the verger was inapatiently rattling his keys. Out of doors the golden sun was shining, and not far from the Church a COM- fortable inn most invitingly stood underneath some giant trees.

"Do you want to return home already ? " asked George, noticing that Tertschka was silently taking the road which led to the town.

"Where else can I go?" she replied, looking up at him.

"There's an inn further on; I think we might allow ourselves some little comfort to-day. Who knows if we shall ever be together again?" " I will stay if you wish it,' she said, pensively. "The foreman will no doubt scold us if we are late. Yet you are right—who can tell if we shall ever walk together again?" They accordingly went up to the inn, which stood at the top of a slight hill. A gigantic beech overshadowed a row of clumsy tables and benches. But nobody was sitting there; everything was hushed and silent. Indoors, however, the people seemed very busy.- The landlord at last looked out of one of the windows. The sleeves of his shirt were of snowy whiteness, a green velvet cap covered his head. He came out at last, looking surprised at being summoned by such strange-looking guests. At George's request he brought them wine in a large glass with handles to it, and bread and meat. Putting all this on the table before them, he asked them to pay at once what they owed, and then hastened back to the house. George pushed over the plate to Tertselika, who carved the meat into small portions. They divided the bread into two parts and set about their common meal. The landlord having provided for one person only, Tertschka made the knife serve her a, a fork. They sipped their wine, by turns, out of the same glass. At last, having done their meal, George lit his pipe, and sat down to enjoy the sight a the blue smoke curling up into the sunlit air. " Look Tertschka," he said, "we would not even have dreamt yesterday that we should be sittitm so cosilv to:lether to-day."

"No, indeed," she sait, "I never could have hoped for it." i , Noon was dr4Wing 00, dby and by 4 merry: xit 'sotind'e were to be heard , a dititance, _ The landlord hastened out. " Tli ride and briktgi °ore are coming," he said to the servants' who ''ere following "and the tables are not set. Be quick - and get them done I." - ' His orders were executed instantly, for there was no time to be lost. Accompanied by the youngsters of the village a stately procession came into eight. Headed by a few musicians were a young couple, their kinsfolk after them, and last of all a whole troop of curious onlookers. The tables were soon taken possession of, and now revelling, drinking and rejoicing were the orde; of the day. The musicians had brought their violins with them, and they set about fiddling with so much energy as almost to take away their breath. strange and varying Were the thoughts which stole over George and Tertschka. At first they looked at the motley crowd- with amazement, Tertschka not being able to keep her eyes off the bride. This lady looked smart enough, there was no question, for being, the daughter of. a rich peasant, she wore a velvet bodice which.made her slender form look* to advantage. A gold chain was twined five or six times round her neck. Her light hair hung down her back in' two long plaits. The wreath of roses on her -head formed a little crown over her somewhat proudish, severe-looking features. The bridegroom was a manly young fellow. Contrary to the habits of his race a dark slender moustache adorned his 'upper lip. His smart hunting cap, which bore a feather and a chamois beard, was quite worthy of George's ad- miration. But by and by an anxious oppressive feeling overcame both of them as they stood amidst the wedding throng, some of whom began examin- ing them with no look of kindness, and appeared. to be thinking "What are those two doing here?" . ~ .. . ..

"Do come‘f Let us go," said Tertschka' at last, "we are no fit company for these. Let us sit down yonder, near the wood; we can look at everything from there, and shall also be able to hear the music."

•George agreed, and they went to the dark pine trees and the sparkling meadow edging it. There they sat down, listening to the soft tunes which the breeze carried over to them. Suddenly, all became silent. They saw the crowd rise from the benches and form a semi-circle. Immediately after the violins were once more heard.

"It's the bride and bridegioom dancing," whispered Tertschka. And so it was, In mea- sured time and with graceful turnings the slender figures were seen to move over the grass. How happily they dance," Tertschka con- tinued, unconsciously leaning on George's shoulder for a better glimpse. "They are indeed happy," he said, but without looking, as if he were speaking,in a dream.' If only we, too, could marry some day or other !" "Go to," she said, bashfully, plucking some flowers at her feet. " Thesi," he went on, for the first time calling her by her name, hi 3 trembling hand bashfully reaching round her waist, " Thesi. I love you so much." .10i.umg, but in her eyes, when she lifted them up to• look at him, he, could read a promise of bliss. And when from the distance the music grew still louder, and the bride and bride- groom, excited by unanimous congratulations and s furious clapping of hands, were whirling round, George drew Tertschka closely to his heart, and their lips met in a long and fervent kiss.

PART 111.

Am I, who undertook to tell you this story in all its simple truthfulness, am I now to try to describe the blessedness, which from that day forward possessed the hearts of George and Tertschka ? I think I may be spared the task, not only because no words can paint that feeling, which all at once had revealed to them the full glory, the unfathomable riches of life but also, because everyone, doubtless, has himself felt the enchantment of love, and the throbbings of his own heart will so much better enal)le him to sympathise with the happiness of those7wo lives. . . . . .

They, indeed, were obliged carefully to hide their happiness as a critninal does his crime. But so much more beautiful did it live and blossom in the deepest recesses of their hearts. Their habits of sobriety, practised their lives through, made them feel it happiness to smile at each other whenever ;they met, or stealthily to shake each other's hand. It seemedmoreover, as if the foreman's watchfulness over them by and bye grew less and less, in conse- quence of which their apprehension least he should hear of, or suspect their late excursion, more and more subsided. George even ventured, when he was obliged to fetch gravel in his wheel-barrow from above the stone pit, to join Tertschka for some minutes, and then the lovers in a long embrace forgot the whole world. It was in one such moment that heavy footsteps suddenly drew near. In terror they broke from each other. The foreman was behind, his face distorted with spite and fury. "So I've caught you at last," he shouted. "You keep disobeying me, thinking I don't notice it. Didn't I know you were strolling about together all day last Sunday, but I wanted to catch you in the very act; and now I'll make you whimper for it." On this he fell upon George from behind, flinging him some feet along the ground, with such strength as to make the sand and dust whirl about.

"Fall, you gallow's dish," he said, "get your bundle and off with you. If ever I chance to see your face again I'll beat your flesh off your bones." Then he shoved George who with difficulty had risen, towards his wheelbarrow, and menacingly clenching his fist at him, pushed him down the hill. He then turned to Tertschka. At first he merely eyed her over with a provoking and cruel look, then he said at last: " tell you what I think later, you good-for-nothing impudent wench," and murmuring indistinct words to himsell he stalked away. . . .

Bewildered and nearly out of his senses, George reached his fellow workers. He mechanically turned over the contents of his wheelbarrow, and then sitting down on some atones he vacantly stared in front of him. The morning sky had beer slightly clouded; now it had grown into a sulky greyish day. An autumnal breeze was softly stirring the tops of the trees, and a slight frosty rain was drizzling down. But George did not feel the drops which beat upon his face. Fiery sparks were dancing before his eyes, and a hot perspire. tion crept over his weak frame. By and by a con• scionsness of the injury dealt him grew mighty in his soul and mixed with a burning feeling of the wrong contemplated to himself and Tertschka. He was to be driven away, and they, she and he, to be torn asunder—they so indissolubly united. Who had a right to do this? No one! The more he—till now so timid and resigned—reflected upon it, the more his soul rebelled against such a thing. A sublime power, a holy courage sprang up in his bosom. He would stand up against any power on earth which dared such oppression! His plain features began to assume {ln expression of steadfastness and energy, and his eyes to sparkle with unmixed delight. . . . . .

At last he rose, and to the astonishment of his comrades he rejoined Tertschka, who sat apart, crying. "Do not cry, Thesi," he said, in a deter- mined yet sympathetic voice. She did not answer. He softly lifted up her head. She sobbed on with more violence. "Don't cry," he repeated, "you can't help things happening, but it's for our good, for now I know what I must do." She bent her head. "He has driven me off, and you must go with me." It was if she did not hear him.

"Down yonder in Krain a railway is building," he continued, "we shall find employment there." She slowly shook her head. "You. Won't? Then there's one more thing I could do. I have been told that soldiers who are not fit for active service any longer, may claim a watchman's position. I shall have a petition written for me; maybe I have a chance; and we could get one of the little houses below, and go and live there. And if we can't do that at once,' he added quickly, seeing that Tertschka gave no sign of approval, but only sobbed more violently, "we must put it off. We could work steadily on, and put by all the time as much as possible. But why don't you speak, Thesi ?"- " Good God," she sorrowfully said, " what you propose is good and well-meaning. But you forget that the foreman won't let me go away.' "He must, you are a child no longer. And what has he to do with you? You are like every one else and can go or stay as you please." "Believe me, he won't let me go—still less with, you. I have always kept it from you," she went on after a pause, a burning red spreading over her face, "but now I will tell you all. Even while my poor mother was living, the foreman often wanted to make love to me, but I kept out of his way, and said I would tell my mother. Last summer, one night when he was coming back from the inn, he began again, and said he meant to marry me. I would not hear of it, and he tried to use violence. But I zot the better of him. and

They then slowly /vailed, towards the cot- tage, she full of apprehension of what might happen, he courageous a d hopeful. Passing the. threshold they found tle foreman sitting at a table, occupied with pee g potatoes. He looked at them, somewhat perplexed, • but his surprise immediately turn to rage pnd fury. "What do you want here ?" he shouted, half rising from his seat, and resting the handle of his knife on the table before him as if preparing to fight. "You have given me warning to leave," George said in a quiet tone, "I have come to fetch my things and to tell you that Tertschka is going with me." •

The foreman seemed on the point of rushing upon him, but involuntarily felt intimidated by the quiet yet resolute aspect of George, who stood boldly before him. "I won't answer you," he at last muttered between his teeth. "You need not; Tertschka is free to do as she likes."

The foreman was gasping for breath. "Take what is yours, Thesi," George continued, turning round for his bag, "and then let's be going. ' The foreman's breast was heaving violently. He seemed not to know what to do. But irresolute as he felt, he yet cast a look of hatred at Tertschka who could not hide her terror: She made towards her chest, and then the foreman, rushing upon her, pushed her down into the cellar, the door of which was standing half open. Then he closed it, and put the key into his pocket. "There is my answer," he stammered out, his limbs shaking with agitation. He then sat down at the table, and with an attempt at indifference continued his former occupation. All this had happened so suddenly, s unexpectedly, that George could not possibly have hindered it. He kept his countenance though. Hanging his bag over his shoulder, without any signs of haste, he with slow steps, went up to the foreman. - "Let Tertschka come out," he said quietly. The foreman went on peeling his potatoes. "Let her come out."

The foreman's hand was seen to shake, and when George a third time and still more firmly reiterated his demand, he sprang up, and shaking his fist, shouted :

"Be gone instantly, or else—" "What else ? " George answered, composedly. "I fear you not, though you are stronger than L I was afraid once, but now I shall deal you blow for blow."

The face of the foreman presented an awful sight. Hatred, revenge and cowardice alternately passed over it. He was gasping for breath, and his hands were clutching the air. George saw all this, and grew bolder and bolder.

"-I there—fore advise you to give me what is mine ;" said George, "if not I shall take it by force." At this moment some men entered the cottage. Noon was drawing on. But it was not for this reason only that they had come, they anticipated a " scene, ' with an instinctive feeling that such an occurrence might some day befall them, too. Their presence stimulated the foreman; he felt reassured. His cowardice, of which he felt himself conscious with inward fury, and which he feared would be remarked, turhed into impudent daring. "Do you hear ? " he roared out, addressing the bystanders; "the wretch dares to threaten me because I shut up that wench of his, to hinder her from running off with-. him." "Do not insult us!" cried George, whose blood involuntary began to rise. "We are honest people, and you have no right to shut her up." "I have/a right with that wench; I have cared for her since she was a child."

"So much the more pity!" said George. "I won't say any more, as I want to spare you before these," he said, pointing to the men, who with visible relish were contemplating the coming quarrel. _

"Hear the dog; he to spare me Upon him, and throw him out." They looked at each other irresolutely, yet they did not move. A loud groaning was heard from the cellar.

"You— see," George went on with increasing emotion, "none of them thinks of touching me. I tell you for the last time: let Tertschka out: 11 you don't, two strokes ot my hammer and the door tails to shivers."

"So you thief, you robber, you want to force the door, do you? Out with you, or I will have the police called in." "They're welcome," cried George, firing up, "it will be seen, then, who is right. They'll learn, too, why you are shutting the girl up. Then it will come to light how you've misused her ever since her childhood; how infamously, you've acted towards her; how you've held back her earnings, the little inheritance of her dead mother whose death is on your conscience. Then they'll get to know how badly you are treating the weak and defenceless up here, and how you are feeding upon the earnings of the poor confided to your. care." George involuntarily stopped. The weight and truth of these accusations had made the measure full with the foreman. He lost all self-control. His face was now of a blueish,\death-like paleness. Roaring like a .wounded bull, with his mouth foaming, his eyes starting out of their sockets, he rushed upon George, swinging his knife high in the air. George, having seked his hammer, raised it against the foreman. A hollow sound was heard. The foreman was hit on the breast. He tottered; dark blood gushed out of his mouth, and with a rattling in his throat, he tumbled to the ground. A dead silence ensued for a moment, and a great terror seized upon all present. But George was like David standing by the corpse of Goliath. " Thesi ! Thesi !" he now called, bursting open the door, "come out ! come out! you are free, our tormentor is defeated."

"Merciful Go!" she cried out, looking at the figure which lay near the door. "He's dead !" she went on, wringing her hands. "Oh, George, now they will take you before the magistrates on a charge of murder." "They may, I shall defend the deed. Those here will bear me witness that I did what I did in self-defence—he was about to till me with his knife. Go," he said, addressing them, "and tell all the world that the workman, George Kiiber, has killed the foreman." _ .

It was some time before any of them felt inclined to go on this errand. George and Tertschka, who continued weeping, sat down before the cottage, and George, heightened in his self-esteem by the consciousness of the ,o-reatness of his deed, from time to time caressingly stroked her cheeks. At.last two detectives, together with a constable, arrived on the scene. They had everything told to them, and then talked assiduously to each other. "He must be taken to the court," said the con- stable. "He's on half-pay, and belongs to the Weiner-Neustadt district." AS George proved submissive, no fetters were made use of. The constable, turning to poor -.trembling Tertschka, tried to encourage her. "Never fear," he said, for according to what he had heard things would not turn out really badly. He even con- sented to Tertschka going with them in the con- veyance, which was to take himself and George up to Weiner-Neustadt. So they drove away, with evening coming on, and a dark night it was. The body of the foreman was brought down from the cottage, whilst a seemingly endless rain kept pouring down. A garrison jail, my gentle reader, is a prison like any other in the world, with this difference only : that those inhabiting it wear worn-out uniforms. In such a place one finds individuals possessing every physical and mental characteristic. As all of them belong to the same state, greater harmony reigns here than in any similar place. There is even a certain voluntary discipline to be met with, an acknowledgment of rank and gradation. Yet it goes without saying that such a jail is, and ever will be. a dreary, desolate Place, and, of course, poor

George coma not tees iignt-nearsea stiere. It saucy jailer, accompanied by a constable, had shut him up one night in that dark, over-filled room. As no straw couch had been prepared, he threw himself down on the floor. But he could not sleep. His exul- • tation, the strength of his confidence, had been waning away during the long drive. Now doubts were assailing and torturing him. And when, at last, a pale glimmer of light stole through the latticed window, by and by illuminating the dreary filthy walls, as well as the unpromising faces of his fellow-prisoners, the painfulness of his situation fell heavily upon his heart. He did not feel really afraid of the consequences of his deed. He had, indeed, been provoked—had been forced to fight for his very life. He did not feel exactly con- science-stricken. Yet the image of the killed man rose before his eyes, lying in his own blood, pale and motionless. His heart ached, and he deeply lamented that it had had to come to this. Day after day, week after week passed on, and George neither was summoned to appear before the court, nor did anyone take notice of him, a circum- stance which aggravated the gloom of his thoughts and feelings. He anticipated with anxiety what life henceforward would be to him. He knew nothing about Tertschka, whom he was painfully longing for. She, indeed, poor heart-stricken creature, through the protection of the good- hearted constable, had succeeded in finding shelter that dreary night, and employment, too, where a house was building. But she felt forlorn and desolate; no one would have guessed how much sa who saw her busy at her work, carrying along bricks or boards of mortar.

But at night, when her day's work was done, or on Sundays, she shyly approached the jail, and looked up at every latticed window in the hope that behind one of them she might see poor George's face. Several times she was driven away with harsh words by the sentinel. In her distress she once even ventured so far as to apply to some of the soldiers near the entrance, begging them to tell her in what part of the building George Kiiber was, as she wanted to talk to him. She was laughed at, and had filthy jokes made at her in return. At last a good-natured sergeant took pity on her and promised to find out George and tell him her wishes. But as to talking to him, that could not be allowed unless she got permission from the governor. He recommended her to go to him early in the morning, as later in the day he was never to be found at home. So next morning she got out her woollen jacket and her cotton frock, and went up to the house the sergeant had pointed out to her. There she had to wait a very long time, the person she was in search of being still in bed. It was very cold in the passage where she sat waiting. At last the gentleman made his appearance, dressed very grandly, and hastily asked what she wanted. He did not let her tell all her story, but said that a prisoner never could be spoken to; she must calm herself, the whole affair would soon blow over.

Tertschka went away but little relieved. Week after week went on and nothing was decided. To tell the truth, the governor was a young man who cared more for society than for work, especially when it was a question, of a halt-pay prisoner. Such people being of no great consequence, there was no need to be over speedy in their concerns. The poor girl, feeling her fears grow stronger day by day, once more tried her fortune with her first counsellor. He advised her to apply to the general. He was somewhat stern, yet he had helped many people. She decided to do this. Again she had to wait a long time, not on this occasion in a cold corridor, but in a comfortable anteroom, which she truly felt a benefit. Winter had set in already. At last she heard a clanking, apparently that of sabres. Some officers issued from the general's room looking rather despondent. Shortly after they had departed the door opened again and a stately gentleman with a long grey moustache looked in, and rather sharply asked her what she wanted. But, seeing that she was crying he immediately softened down: He bade her come, and sitting down himself, he silently listened to what she had to say. He then put several ques- tions, and, finally, asked her to tell him the whole story. She did this as best she could, somewhat awkwardly, but with such great simplicity, warmth, and deep feeling, that the general, who kept twisting his great moustache, seemed visibly touched. When she had done he rose from his seat, and softly laying his hand on her shoulder, told her to go home in peace. He gave her his word that the whole affair should now be got through with the shortest possible delay, and, as he hoped, to George's advantage. , Tertschka went away with a lightened.heart, her heaviest cares dispelled. The general paced the room to and fro after her exit, making his spurs jingle from time to time. At last he sent for the governor, requesting him to come immediately. Nevertheless, he had to wait some time before the young man, his face on fire, entered the room. deeply bowing...

"'Sir," said the general, "It is about a month ago that a certain George Kiiber, on half-pay, was brought here for trial." The governor instinctively put his hand to hii forehead.

"George Kiiber ? Yes, yes, it's about some homicide, I think?" . - .

"Quit'e so, and I wish the trial gone through with as quickly as possible." "Oh, of course, nothing more easy," said the other, visibly relieved; "it's a thing that happens often enough with that sort of people. The fellow has to run the gauntlet once or twice and all is done with."

" That is not my opinion, my dear sir," replied the general, "that would be but a very superficial proceeding. On the contrary, I want the affair to be looked into, as quickly as possible, it's true, but none the less with all care and circumspection. Without interfering with your sagacity as a judge, I venture to remark that in this case there are very peculiar circumstances involved." _ _

Saying which the general frowned, and the governor, knowing what that meant; bowed and left the room. He immediately repaired to his office, awl-being destitute' neither of ability nor of perspicacity, much time really did not elapse before George as well as the witnesses, with Tertschka among them, were summoned to appear. The following verdict was announced: "George Kfiber, half-pay, of the Twelfth Regiment, is found to be guilty of homicide, but understanding that he has been driven to this 'by necessity of self-defence as well as by the weight of some explanatory circum- stances, and considering his reproachless conduct in the army, the time ot enquiry. is taken as consti- tuting the time of his punishment." The magistrate could not help feeling a little ashamed at his delay when, on the following day, on bringing the general the verdict for his signature, that gentleman, after having read it through, Apped his shoulder and said: "It sometimes happens, as on this occasion, that a little negli- gence in one's duty may yet have its good conse- quences." Two days later the general summoned George and Tertschka to his presence. He silently con- templated them for a while, asked them several questions, and advised them to stay on in the town for some time longer. He would provide for them by getting them suitable employment, and they would hear from him later. They expressed their thanks in their bashful way, and went away. When left alone the general paced the room to and fro. Strange thoughts were moving him. Many years ago he had been in love with a fair girl. Because of her, he became very unhappy. True, she (lid not repulse his love—his proud and valiant young soul would certainly have borne up under that. But his purest and most sacred feel- ings had been played with and misused. This filled him with bitterness, with a somewhat nervous contempt of women in general, and he liked to make a certain boast of it. He pretended not to believe in the existence of love, which he main- taind was only to be found in romances, the fictions of the deranged brains of poets—never in reality itself. And now, after having fostered this opinion for long with something like passion, even against the protests of his own heart, and those of his friends to whom he had communicated this opinion, he unexpectedly met with its confutation in all its depth, tenderness and devotion, in these two forlorn creatures. He felt at the same time ashamed and deeply moved, a touch of envy mixing with these feelings. He resolved, however, as far as he could do so, to make these two young people happy for the rest of their lives.

In the vicinity of the Castle of Ehrenlausen, which looks down from its wood-grown eminence into a village ,of the eame name on the rushing Mur, a line of rails lies amid the green pastures and meadows, a lonely signal box standing by oral of the curves. ELECTRICITY v. THE STEAM

LOCOMOTIVE.

The fact, says " Fielden's Magazine," in an article dealing with the development of eiectric traction, that an electric service is always run at more frequent inter- vals, is the outward sign of an important truth. With ' the horse, the man, or the steam locomotive, the power that can be exerted is strictly limited by the ability to carry sufficient store of energy -producing material, or to provide sufficiently frequent replenishment. With electric driving, the power that can be exerted is limited only:by the ability to distribute energy in such form that it can be taken up by the locomotive in Passing, and this is only limited by the pressure that can be used in the distribution system, which is again only limited by the poasibilities of insulation, As the possibilities of insulation are increasing almost daily, It may be said that for practical purposes the possibilities of power for electtic driving are unlimited, and consequently the possibilities of the railway service of the future, so far as power is concerned, are un- limited, or merely depend upon the working out of practical details, such as have been worked out in other directions with uniform success. There can hardly he any doubt therefore that the supersession of the system of driving our railways by steam locomotives will only continue as long as it is convenient to engineers, while they make their asrangetrienta for displacing them, or as long as financial reasons dictate.

Elizabeth Jonas, 39, Princess-street, said the prisoner. who was very drunk, walked into her house and out again. She fcllowed him and saw him assault the polio officer.

Elizabeth Bennison,Pidacess-street, said the prisoner also came into her house and she told him to go out. She saw the assault en be police sergeant. _

Prisoner said this was a made-up affair. The elsairraan said the evidence in the theft case was not sufficient and it would be dismissed. Prisoner would have to go to gaol for a month for the drunkenness, and at four mmths for the assault.

THE SCHOOLLBOARD AND DR. WILLIAMS' PINK PILLS.

A Testimonial as 'Evidence.

At the Glasgow Justice of Peace Court, Mr. Bernard' Keir, residing at 55, Wynfore-street, was charged with failing to provide elementary educa-• bort for his daughter Frances. He pleadei pot guilty. Mr. Gerrard, School hoard officer, deposed that out of 203 times when the school was open the child was pre- sent only 82 times. _ _

The father : lam quite willing to give her all the education she requires. She ha l been kept from school owing to her bad health. Questioned by the Bench, the child said, "I like to go to school, sir, and my father and mother wish me to go but I was not whll."

rat,The prcsecutor : I have here a copy of a newspaper report containing an article headed "Wonderful Re- covery from S. Vitus' Dance by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills." It refers to Francea Kerr, and states that the is quite well. The newspaper article • was put in. It stated that the child had been cured of St. Vitus' Dance, and a twitching of the shoulder and bead, by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. The Chairman and his trother justice read the account, which proved so clearly that Dr. Williams' pink pills bad cured the child that the p osecutor had treated the newspaper articla as evidence in this ease.

The Chairman, addressing Mr. Kerr, raid : We must impress upon you the necessity of keeping your girl at school until she i 9 fourteen years of age. But we believe thac your girl had been suffering from a nervous trouble, and we are gad to think that she is better now. Will you promise to send her regularly to school in the future ?

Mr. Kerr : Yes, your honour. The Bench inflicted a nominal fine of five elui?lings.

OROWNED IN LATCHFORD LOCKS.

The Precautions for Public Safety.

A sad drowning fatality occurred at Latchford Locks about eleven o'clock on Saturday night. It appears that Thomas Ashton, a timekeeper in the employ oE Messrs Bennie and Tnompspri, contractor., who resided at 281, Tbelwall—lane, was crossing the locks when bee slipped and fell into the water. A man named Bate. who was near at the time saw him fall, and gave an alarm. In response to his shout, one of the lothiners came upon the scene with a lifebelt, but was unable to see Ashton. Sandy Woodward, a diver, was sent for, and after about two or three hours' searching succeeded in recovering the body. It transpired that Bate saw the deceased asleep by a wall near the lock. Be aroused him and told him to go home by the road. Deceased, however, tried to cross the lock, but fell into the water and was drowned.

TEIE INQUEST.

THE JURY AND THE LIGHTING OF THE LOCKS.

The inquest was conducted by Mr a C Yates, coroner, at the Dog and Dart Hotel, on TuQeiav afternoon. Mr Ntwerian, solicitor, represented the Manchester Ship Canal Company, while Captain Waring was alao present.

_ Deceased's wife stated that her husband left Eome at about five o'clock on Saturday evening, but did not ray where he was going. She did not bear of the unfortu- nate occurrence until five o'clock on Sunday morning. Questioned by the Coroner, witness said her hueband knew the locks very well. Ile had raver expressed any intention to take his life. He tcok a little drink occa- sionally. Henry Bate, Cedar-terrace, Grappenhall, said that at 10 20 on Saturday night be found the deceased lying in theeroad against a wall. He was asleep, and it war raining. He apprared to be in drink. Witness assisted him on to his feet and asked him where he lived, but he did not answer. Witness's daughter knew him. After deceased had get up, verfnesee started him home by way of the Swing Bridge, remarking: "Whatever you do don't go over those locks." His mem for saying that was that he did not think it safe for a man in drink to go over the locks in the dark. Witness watched - him up the road and saw him stop near the hig gate. Veitnesa next saw him making for the leek and ran after him. On arriving at the big gate be saw Ashtem going to the left of the little locks. He shouted and ran to the locks to stop him, but before he could reach him Ashton bad fallen into the water. Witness could not see him, and he called to the lockmen, who immediately turned on the lights and brought the grapples. Ashton did not appear again. Henry Davies, lock gateman, said that on bearing cries of distrese he switched on the electric lights at the locke. On looking into the small lock he saw deceased's hat and bubbler; on the water. No one came to the top and they started grappling straight away, but the body was not recovered until 2.30 on Sunday morning. The depth of the water was 42e feet. It was a very dark nighr.—Questioned by the Coroner, witness said deceased had a perfect right to get across the locks. 0 .. . was a public way. The intermediate gates were opeo• They had no footpath attached to them. They,eggie - expecting a yeesel. Mr Smith (foreman of the jury) observed that th& intermediate lock gates were the proper ones for the public to go over. He wanted to know whether there was not a rule that, when the intermediate gates were open, one of the officials should ba on duty t warn the , public of the danger. Witness said there was no rule to that e oct. He 1 , e added that the platform connected with the intermedi- ate gates was out of order. Captain Waring had told ' them to keep a lookout, and if anyone wanted guiding over the locks they would do 97. Teey were not sup- posed to stand on the way all the time, The Coroner, in summing up, said a little point had been made, and it had resolved itself into a lather big point he though.. From what he could gather the gatee where Ashton met his death were out of order and under repair, and the foreman of the jury naturally asked if , any directions had been given to protect the public who bad been accustomed to go that way and might not have known of the damaged gr.tes. They understood , certain instruction had been given to the lockmen to keep a lookout, but be did not think that was sufficient. - He thought it was • pity the Canal Company did not ' en nights like that—Saturday nights—place a man pur- posely to prevent the public getting into danger. Be , was quite sure the Canal Company would do their best - to meet any recommendation the jury might have to A make.

--Mr Newman said if tEe public could confine them- selves to one set of gates he did not think there would he much trouble in safeguarding almost everybody. Fie had been ta the locks that day and it was the only thing be had to .suggest.

,

Mr Smith remarked that the Parish Council bad' repeatedly wiitten to the Canal Company asking them to light the locks, but they had ignored every letter sent. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe P. S. A. Society.

There was an average attendance at the rneeticg of this Society on Sunday afternoon in Wycliffe Church. Mr Bannister occupied the chair, and in a brief address spoke upon Christ's great love to man. Mr Heaton, of Manchester, was the speaker for the afternoon, and interested his hearers with aa address upoa the Rose of Sharon, from which he drew a useful leseon.—Mr R H Pearson contributed in his uqually acceptable manner two violin solos entitled " Broken Melody " and " Sono of Love." The service was alio takes part in by Mr B Farrar (hoc. secretary), Mr Owen and Mr W BeSqvick, jun.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel. O. Sunday last the Rev Gardham Reed, the newl7 arrived minister, preached morning and evening, before large congrigations. In the evening be took for his teat the words " What think ye of Christ "(Matt. 22 c. 42 v.), and preached a most intellectual and instrucive sermon, before an attentive and appreciative congrega- tion. Both his sermons gave evidence of the preacher's undoubted ability, and showed pl tidy to the members of the Chapel that there had come amongst tthem a capable leader, and a man of no mean intellectual •capacity. During the evening service Miss Alice Bridge gave an effective rendering ot Gonnod's " Entreat me not to leave Thee" in her uthal accomplished manner. and the choir rendered "Achieved is the g lorioua work" from " The creation" (Haydn) conducted by Mr A fl.pruce (choir master), Mr Fred Podmore M.1.11.M. pre- sided at the organ.

Golborne-street BaptiGt Church. There was a large congregation in the above place of worship oa Sunday morning last, when the Rev. Ehnen Dormice gave his third lecture on "Christian Verities," the subject being " Does God :yds!: ?" The discourse was listened to with rapt attention by a congregation which included many strangers. The preacher said that this question was the most important question in the realm of thought, because on no other theory than that of God's existence could any one of the problems of light _ Or thought be explained. The very idea of God was an intellectual •neessonity (they might call Sim by any name they liked) because whenever they tried to account for the existence of the world or the things in it they were driven back by sheer intellectual necessity to the words of their text "In the beginaing was God." Religion had been defined " as the recognition ef an essential relationship between the human soul and a per- ins' God." This definition presupposed an acquaint- ance with the idea of God's existence Again they bad proved in a previous lecture that man universally pos- sessed a religions faculty and if there ware no God then they were driven to the conclusion that the noblest faculty man pos essed was not only useless but a ccn- etant mockery to him. If this faculty had any counter- part—and it was contrary to reason to suppose it had nct—then that counterpart was God. A certain stheol of sceptical philosophers had tried te overcome this difficulty by throwing doubts as to tho 'validity or trustworthiness of the faculties,hut to follow that argument to its logical conclusion would lead us to doubt the existence of all things inclusive of ourselves. Such an admission would affect cur knowledge of the ; physical world quite as much as it affected our know- ledge of the spiritual world, Be did not hesitate to assert that God was just as real to the consciousness of the man who loved Him as the physical universe to the man of science. To say that the existence of God coul I not be proved was a feeble and ignorant argument. could anything be proved ? Before we could have an evidence or experience of anything we were compelled to make the most tremendous unproven assumptions. The preacher then spoke upon several other arguments for the existence of God viz. The cosmological ontological, and teleological and con- cluded his discourse by asking his hearers to treat this question in a rational naanner.To accept the existence of God might possibly involve one or two minor difficulties but to deny His existence increased those difficulties a milliootolci• If only able men could be brought to a knowledge of:God anclicould know Him not only In:study but in life and love, the murmurs of the world would 'surely sink into silence and the troubled heart of man weuld find peace. The chapel was crowded at the evening service when the pastor again preached

PRESENTATION OF WAR

CLASPS TO WARRINCiTON VOLUNTEERS.

A very interesting ceremony took place at Parkbouse Camp, Salisbury Plain. on Saturday when a numbar of the members of the first and second contingents of War- rington Volunteers received the long delayed clasps for their South African medals. The presentation was made in the presence of the whole battalion be Colonel J C Ridgway, V. 1)., commanding the Ist V.B. (P.W.V.) South Lancashire Regiment. There are four bars, beer. ing the names, Laings Nek, Orange River Colony, Transvaal ani Natal. The recipients were under the command of Captain Appleton and Captain Cary, and numbered about thirty. In makiag the presentation, the Colon(' said that he would have liked the Brigadier (Colonel M Qualt Jones) to have distributed the clasps, bat he had had to leave the camp early that morning. He, however thought the men would value the clasps no lee" for having them, from the hands of their own Colsnel. It would have pleased him to have had the clasps before, but the War Office had delayed in sending them, and it was owing to the interference of Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P.. that they had received them even now.—The Colonel referred in sympathetic terms to the death of Private Barker, and said be hoped that as many men as possible would attend his funeral the following day. Re had been pleased with the behaviour of the battalion in camp, and he believed they had made a very favourable impression on the Brigadier and Sir Evelyn Wood. In conclusion, the Colonel paid a passing reference to the illness at the King, who, he was delighted ta hear, was rapidly gaining strength. Re then called for three cheers for his Majesty, which were heartily given. Tne band, under the conductorship of Mr J T Richardson, then played the National Anthem, and the men wore dismissed to prepare for the return home.—lt about 5.30 p.m. the battalion assembled on the parade ground to start for Warrington. An enthusiastic " send off " was accorded them by the members of tlae other batta- lions in camp, the band of the Cheshire Regiment play- ing the battalion out of camp. Two trains were reqMsit:oned to convey the men home, the right-half battalion starting just before 9 n'click and the left-half battalion about an hour later, Warrington being reached at 5.30 and 7.30 respectrvely.

A SrdANGE STANDARD OF VALUE.

Captain Barberie. an Anglo-Indian of infinite humour into whose short life of thirty-five years was crowded more incident than falls within the experience of most centenarians,bad the misfortune to lose a leg at the seige of a city in India, and was awarded a Gavernment annuity of £5O. Thereafter, the Captain adopted a stendard of value reculiar t 3 himself,end would refer to £lOO as two legs, £25 as half a leg, and so on. It is not to every portion of the human body that Captain Barberie's system could be applied. A human being may lose a leg, or even two lees, and yet live ; but no government has ever been called upon to allow a Man a pension because he has lost his 7stomach. big liver, or his heart in its service. (We use this last expression in its physical and not its romantic sense). The loss of those necessary parts of 'our being means death ; their derangement, misery. Hundreda of thousands coull from .bitter experience tall us as much, but for the presentwe will be content to listen to what Mrs. Annie McDonald, of Homewood Villa, Winstonley Crescent. Ramsgate, has to say. "It was *at the age of 21," writes Mrs McDonald, "that I was first attacked by indigestion. It began in the usual way—s feeling of pain and discomfort after food,hlig ht at first,but gradualy increasing in intensity until it became veritable torture, and I was afraid to eat even so much as a piece of bread and batter. I thought it would pass away of itself, but instead it daily grew worse. and I was compelled to seek medical advice. I went to one of the great London hospitals, where I was questioned and sounded, and sent home with a bottle of cod li,ver oil, being in- structed to eat the most nourishing food end take plenty of fresh air. It was impossible for me to follow this advice, for I bad no appetite and was, besides afraid to eat. The cod liver oil I forced myself to take ; but it was rejected immediately, and I could never succeed in keeping lion my stomach. This difficulty I explained to the doctors' who merely told me that I must persevere with it, Finding so relief, I tried three other hospitals in succession, but continued to grow worse. I became thinner and thinner, and with sunken eyes and drawn face at the age of 26 appeared like an old woman. For five years I suffered this misery, the pain I endured being only too apparent in my face. Some people were of opinion that I was consumptive, and told me so. The fear that I should die and leave my little ones quite unnerved me. We went to Brighton to live, and while there I experi- enced one of my worst attacks. It was on that occa- sion I was advised by a neighbour to try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup ; she had used it herself, and, knowing lie worth, was convinced it would do me good. I obtained a balf-erown bottle of it, and never was money better spent. At the end of a week I was a different woman—l felt better and brighter, and my appetite revived. Three other half-crown bottles completed the work which the first had begun, and ever since my health his been as good as I could wish it to be."

OWNED SCHOOLS.

Sir John Brunner's Views.

Writing to the " Times," Sir J T Brunner, in a letter dated Thursday last week, says : Last night Mr Balfour told us something about his new clause to provide for the appointment of trust managers of voluntary ChOMEI. 'THE LYCEUM

FIR HENRY IRVING'S FAMOUS THEATRE WHOSE FART HANGS IN THE HALANCE. •

its historical landmarks, but in the theatrical world it is looked upon as almost as iconoclastic a change if Sir Henry Irving's name and presence should cease to be associated with the mote of his life's success. To the present generation his personality is indissoluble with the Lyceum; and people are apt to forget that other famous names have been written upon the 1.610 of this theatre, such as those of Charles Matthews. From the seventies the history of the Lyceum is the tale of Sir Henry Irving and Miss Ellen Terry, his famous coadjutrix, and their productions, which have been constantly revived, are familiar to every playgoer.

Nowadays people do not go to Cowes Regatta so much to see the racing, although the sport is always good, as for the social pleasures of the meeting. THE KAISER.

THE KING OF ITALY.

AN INTERESTING MEETING OF EUROPEAN MONARCHS.

general celebrations and illuminations, and the Otter den Linden has been dressed in its best. The most amusing point in reference to the meeting, of the Czar and Kaiser is the chagrin such an vent always raises in the jealous mind of the French, who have grown to look upon the Czar as their special perquisite amongst foreign royalties and always resent any act of friendliness on his part, especially towards Germany. At the moment the general aspect of European politics is in a most interesting state, though outwardly at least the international relations of the vaTious Powers are very friendly. These, however, vie know from past experience may be totally altered in the twinkling of an eye by the cropping up of some small question.

Mr. Balfour's statement in the House of Commons with regard to the Commission of Inquiry into the War has been a good deal dis- cussed and much criticised. There can be no doubt about it that there• is a strong suspicion in many quarters that the inquiry will be a • farce, and the Government will have some difficulty in con- vincing the nation that the whole conduct of the War \ from beginning to end will be investi- gated as the country con- siders it ought to be. However, only the chair- man of the Com- mission has been appointed up to the present, and perhaps when the names of his colleagues ar e forthcoming, and the terms of re- ference have been definitely decided upon, a little more confidence will be mani- fested. The task which the Commission will be called upon to undertake is a stupendous one, and not altogether enviable. It will not be an easy matter to find men possessing the requisite qualifications fer the work. It seems almost impossible to select even seven men, as Mr. Balfour suggests, who would give entire satisfaction to everybody, but we must wait as patiently as may be, and see what the choice of the Government will be. The 'post of Chairman especially will be one of great responsibility and difficulty, and will involve ardous labours. Lord Elgin is to be ,commended for his public spirit in serving the ,country in this capacity, and let us hope that we Ishall find in him the man of intellectual grasp,

and ashore when the weather is fine, and the sun shines. There is light and life and motion every- where, and everybody seems full of happiness. Cowes has been even gayer than usual this year, being Coronation week and the King and Queen and so many other Royalties and distinguished people being present. The centre of interest during the week has been the new Victoria and Albert, not only on account of his Majesty being aboard her, but because this is her first appearance amongst the miscellaneous fleet which annually assembles off the picturesque island. IS THE BIBLE TRUE?

11 EAD "The Truth of God demonstrated by the British Nation," by W. H. Masos,which demonstrates at once the t tith of God and lila Word and the facts that the British are Israeli, not Jews, and that our Monarch ts David's heir and God's Vicegerent on the Throne of Israel. Publishers, ROBERT BANKS dc 60N, Racquet Court, Fleet- street, London.

OuR Constitution is, after The Democratic all, a very rough machine Machine.

for the expression of the national will. It ought to be impossible for a Premier to persevere, as Mr. BALFoun is doing, in such an enterprise as the Education BM after such a verdict as the North Leeds election con- veyed. Theoretically a seat has been transferred from one party to the other, and that is all. In point of fact, however, this bye election was really a referendum on the Education Bill. The experi- ment was carried out under the most favourable conditions for the Government. North Leeds has been a safe Conservative stronghold ever since 1885, before the Home Rule split, and the Unionists secured a majority of 2,517 it the last election in 1900. And yet a Liberal has been I returned by 758 votes. There is no reason to suppose that the result would have been different had the experiment been made in Manchester or Sheffield. The inference from Bury and North Leeds is irresistible. The majorities which Mr. BALFOUR musters night after night in the House of Commons with all the machinery of the new rules and the closure are simply groups of private persons who have ceased to represent the con- stituencies which sent them to Parliament in blissful ignorance of the coming Educations Bill. There is no constitutional remedy. In theory Mr. BALFOUR may go stolidly on for his full term of seVen years, adding measures yet more obnoxious to the bad record of the Corn Tax and the Education Bill. As yet he shows no disposition whatever to bow to the popular will. He rejects all hints of compromise, and persists in his scheme for endowing denominational schools, and alienat- ing public money from public control, as though the opinion of the electorate were a sheer irrelev- ance. The Constitution can be made to work only with the loyal goodwill of the statesman at the helm. Mr. Baia-unit unlucirily inherits Lord SALISBURY'S contempt for democracy. It is a short-sighted policy,and Mr. BALFOUR is tempting a dangerous reaction. He may carry b s Bill this autumn, but he will simply be providing the next Liberal majority with work to undo.

With Mr. BALFOUP:S /*O- A Fair Compromise. jection of Mr. DaLox's

compromise perished the last hope that the Education Bill can be hammered into a moderately fair working scheme. Even the most sweeping amendments would leave it a re- actionary measure. But Mr. DILLON'S suggestion would at least have removed the gross injustice , which it threatens to the Nonconformists in the 8,000 parishes where the Church school is the only school. Mr. BALFOUR'S plan is to leave these schools in the hands of their own trustees, who appoint four members to the Board of Manage- ment. A minority of two were to represent the parish and the parents, with the inestimable privilege of uttering impotent criticisms on the expenditure of the money which is to be found by the ratepayers and the Exchequer. Mr. Dria,ox's suggestion was to reverse the proportions, leaving two members to the trustees, two to the parish council, and two to the parents. But Mr. BALFOUR would hear nothing of this compromise. He can, he de- dares, consent to nothing which would destroy what he calls the " denominational character" of these schools. They are still to be what his relative, Lord Huun CECIL, described as "schools with two doors." The child is forced into the first door by the State—the second leads to the church. It is a naive scheme,and it might have succeeded within limits, if only Mr. BALFOUR had not himself taken the great step which destroys for ever the "de nominational character" of these very schools, He has made them what they were only.in name before

—" national schools," since the public is to find the money for the education which they provide. That step once taken there can be no going back,and the logical consequence of public control must follow. It is a thousand pities that the attention of the country should be concentrated perforce on ex" ternal details of this kind. For the real problem for Great Britain is how to provide a really eta' cient training for the youth of all classes, who are simply growing up a generation or two behind their alert and scientific competitors on the Con- tinent. From the Indian Army has just come a, report on the education of its younger officers which informs us that they can make no progress in their professional studies until they have firs been subjected to the sort of discipline which a ' boy in the sixth standard of a good Board School would rather resent. Elementary arithmetic, geography, and English history are the recondite subjects in which these young gentlemen have to be instructed after they have obtained the KiNcis commission. That throws an ugly light on the future of our Army, but the brothers of these wail' born illiterates who will control our commerce, our engineering, our manufactures, are they in a better plight? Such a state of things would be quite impossible in France or Germany, where the youth who fails to pass an examination much " stiffer than the London Matriculation is

doomed to three years' service in the ranks of the conscript army,whilst his better-educated brethren escape with one.

ONE cannot help sus" Lord Rosebery and Ireland. pecting that Lord itosP• nERY was for once behind the times when he confined his reference to the

Irish question, in his recent speech to the "Liberal League," to a beggarly declaration that Ireland shall never have an independent Parliament. That is a safe but a meaningless remark, which has the merit of offending nobody, since not even *Mr' REDMOND claims anything of the kind. Bat the repetition of phrases of this sort conveyg the impression that the attitude of Liberals is

simply one of negation and refusal. Eve° Mr. BALFOUR would like to "kill Home Rule with kindness." Lord ROSEBERY merely ignores it fig an unpopular subject. But the tide is turning' Mr CATHCART WASON, the Unionist member for the Orkneys, has just changed sides because 118 has discovered—after a visit to the West of Ire- land in Mr T W RUSSELL'S company—that Uniorr ism will never curs its misery or redress its wrongg, The case of SERGEANT SHERIDAN has touched 511

our consciences in the last few week He was the product of a system which regarded the police not as the protectors and guardians of the community, but/simply as the servants of the executive, employid to repress the majority and uphold the privileges of the landlord caste. That evil all admit. Yet the system of a' centralised police and packed juries—a daily 01 normal coercion—is more or less inevitable untl we concede Home Rule, and so enlist the syn3lo- - of the majority on the side of law and order' Take again the Laud Question. The only solu- tion lies in the compulsory buying out of the land lords. But what English Government would dare to spend the necessary money? Ireland is imPe" tent, for Ireland has not the right possessed 11 every petty corporation at home to pledge It" 4 credit to raise money for a public purpose. Wltilt self-government that difficulty would vanish a' once.
