There is another instance of the want of operation between committees disclosei in agenda for Tuesday's meeting of the Council. On the minutes of the Paving 3°4_ Sewerage Committee there is a resolution reque ing the Tramways Committee to set back the Pr,: along the south side of Church-street to allow the widening of the carriageway of that street. is not unreasonable to ask if the Paving 9,12„ , Sewerage Committee could net have foreseen I,"' necessity for this improvement before the PI were erected ? The Tramways Committee viol' seem to have the right here to request in rotor° that the Paving and Sewerage Committee pay Or, cost of the removal and re-erection of the poles: J" is their fault that the work has to be done twlee' Of course, whichever committee eventually Ws' the ratepayers are the losers in the end, and WY are entitled to ask for and expect better manage- rnent of their affairs than is here disclosed.

The full story of the shortage of water in the town and the remedial measures adopted is told ill the minutes of the Water Committee. On the 30th of June the Engineer reported on the scarcitY of water, and warning notices were issued. Thence, mattess developed with dramatic sudden- ness. On tho 2nd of July a representative of Messrs. J. Simpson and Co., Ltd., engineers, atten- ded before the Committee, and on the same dal the firm received an order to supply and erect the necessary appliances for the purpose of supplYing the new reservoir at Winwick with water fro' the No. 1 shaft at Delph-lane. Eight dal! later Messrs Simpson reported that they hee started the first temporary pump, and in the course of another 18 hours they were deliveria water at Wit.vvick reservoir. On the 14th Jul/ the committee instructed the Town Clerk t° express their satisfaction to Messrs Simpson, wiles from all accounts fully deserved this commendkr tion. The task they had accomplished was la° small one, involving as it did the chartering of e special train from London to convey the urgentlY required appliances. Furthermore the coit been very considerable, but it could hardly have been avoided seeing that the demand for mita 1.1.14

has so rapidly increased. Another few montli„s grace would have reloived the committee of anxiety for the permanent plant at Delph-1500 would then have been in operation. It is well, indeed, that the Water Committee embarked their scheme of extension when they did, for 600 twelve months delay would have been so serioos that one does not care to contemplate the conslY quences.

There is no progress to report in connectior! with the tramway crossing at Wilderspool. Abou. a month ago the Londonand North-Western Rall' way Company commenced their portion of VA ` work by removing. some overhead wires, and hoe , ran high that the job would soon be completed' , Since then, however, nothing has apparently be°? done, and notwithstanding the strongest possibl representations from the Corporation, best that can be got from the Company is a stare; ment to the effect that the work will be complete" in a few weeks." It is already about 18 mont,llls ' since the Corporation first made overtures to 06 ' Railway Company for the carrying out of th : i work, and correspondence has ncver cease ti the subsequent period, the Railway COO? pany maintaining throughout a most ille°l3; . siderate, non-committal policy. The time weel'e„f , is so out of proportion to the extent the operations required that one cannot help WI seek reasons for, the company's dilatoriness. TheY , seem to have no desire to help the Corporation 1 ' the town in any way, and it will be remember° I that they acted the part of obstructionists to tlzi,,_° , Corporation when the extension of the boroug': , took place in 1896, and also fought the CorPera ' tion Tramways Order in both Houses of Parbws- ment. It is quite evident that Warrington Wl_ no friend in the London and North-Western way Company, and the passing of the resolutwq below is a very proper remonstrance on the part u the General Purposes Committee.:— c. "That this Committee express strong dissatisfa

tion at the unnecessary delay of the London and North-Western Railway Company in carry- ing out the alterations for the Tramways at the level crossings at Wilderspool, and request the Company to cause the works to be completed as soon as practicable:'

The action of the London and North-Western Railway Company in cancelling during the recent holidays their excursion fares to most of the sea- side resorts has seen the subject of much adverse criticism. The public have certainly a grievance against the company. For many weeks past the hoardings have been covered with placards announcing cheap excursions to various parts but not a word has been published that it was the intention of the company to cancel them during the holidays and for Coronation Day. The result has been that many have proceeded to the booking office with a view of obtaining an excursion ticket and have been mortified to find that the clerk has received instructions not to issue excursion tickets at the very time when they would be most in de- mand. It is due to the travelling public that the same publicity should be given to the cancelling of the excursion fares as is given to their announce- ment or such notices may come to be regarded as a delusion and a snare.

The Warrington and District Agricultural Society's Show at Walton Park yesterday, fully maintained the high reputation it has gained of being one of the best district shows in the country. Not only was there a marked increase in the number of entries but the quality oc. the stock shown was even better than the high level reached last year. Those who remember the show when it was held under the auspices of the Warrington Shire Horse Society cannot fail to be struck with the great improvement which has taken place in the quality of the horses in this district. This improvement is largely, if not entirely due, to the good work which has been steadily carried on during :the past thirteen years by that Society. What nas been done for horses may eventually be realised in respect of cattle, pigs and agricultural produce. These classes have been added to the society's programme during recent years. This year's cattle, were, if anything, a little better ;than last year, and this was more noticeable in re- spect of dairy cows. It is too early to speak of the pig classes for this is the first year they have been included in the list of entries,but as there are several good breeders of pigs in the district these classes should show a great improvement in the near future. It was a great disappointment to find so few entries for eggs, dressed poultry and potatoes. In such a rich agricultural district, keener compe- tition may reasonably be expected. The small -awards offered are perhaps accountable for the naeagre list of entries, but in any case there seems some necessity for taking steps to make agricul- tural produce a more popular feature of the show.

Mr. J. W. Knowles cm. Wednesday appeared in tl e peculiar position of solicitor for the defence in a prosecution undertaken by an authority of which he is a member and somewhat of a shining light— the Warrington Rural District Council to wit. Moreover Mr Knowles' objection on a technical point gained the day for the lucky defendants who also obtained costs against the Rural Council. That authority will be puzzled to decide on what basis to assess the value of the services of a mem- ber who gives thorn his advice in Council and then appears against them in Court with advice which costs the district a few guineas.

Before Saturday night there had not been a drowning fatality at Latchford Locks for a con- siderable time, so that the question of the safety of the public who exorcise their right of way at this point has been allowed to drop. The sad occurrence by which a man named Thomas Ashton was drowned in the Locks on Saturday has, how- ever, revived the agitation for a safer thorough- fare over the waterway at this point, and there is no doubt that the demand for some extra precau- tions on the part of the Ship Canal Company, is LJustifieu by the existing facts. Even if a man be drunk it ought to be possible for him to cross the , Locks in safety, whereas a perfectly sober indivi- dual runs great risk at r.resent if the night eir ri).e dar. k or foggy. The least the Canal Company can do is t® light the Locks for the guid- ance of pedestrians, and see that the handrails on the gates are of an efficient, safe description. It is the easiest thing in the world for a person to fall through the existing rails in the centre of the platforin,and we trust that proper measures will be taken to very considerably reduce all possibility of another such accident as that of Saturday night. It is not to the credit of the Canal Company that such indifference is shown with regard to the safety of the public.

" The Trade" in -Warrington is casting enviou eyes on the Lovely-lane district if one may judge by the applications which are to be made at the forthcoming licensing sessions. Mr. Benjamin Kenyon has been allowed for many years to enter the lists alone with his application for a full license for the" Green Man" beerhouse, but this year he is to be accompanied by two other applicants for the magistrates' favour. Mr. E trle of the Green Dragon Inn, Bridge-street has given notice of his mtention to apply for the transfer of his license to premises proposea to be erected in Lovely-lane and Mr. Bolton is also applying for a licence for new premises proposed to be erected in the immediate vicinity. The population of the district is rapidly increasing and we suppose an attempt will- be made to convince the magistrates that the inhabi., tants have not reasonable facilities for obtaining intoxicating liquor. Hitherto the licensing com- mittee has considered several off-licensed premises quite sufficient to meet the needs of the neighbourhood and we cannot .see that the circum- stances of the case have materially altered.

-- Mr. Chamberlain returned to his favourite theme of the hostility of Edrope towards this country in his great City speech. He would have tzs band ourselves together with the Colonies into • a sort of federation against all the rest of the civilised world. It is just this self-conscious aggressive attitude which begets hostility. Mr. Chamberlain is the last man who should talk of these European suspicions as inevitable. They became clamant with • the Jameson Raid, whose authors and contrivers he shielded and whitewashed. They • grew louder when he went out of his way to insult Russia, to threaten France, and to traduce Ger- raspy. They have their root in the sort of feeling which some .of our Jingoes entertain towards Russia. It is said that Russia is so bent on self- aggrandisement that she will break her most solemn pledges. to win a mile of territory. That, unfortunately, is exactly what our enemies say of us. They can point to Egypt, which we have gq oulocitfiee Lordds,w e Yseeckanno promised to evacu.ate. ~T.whee _which preceded the annexa- tion of the Transvaal. It is the Disraeli-Chamber- lain policy, and not the . mere existence of the British Empire, which excites European hostility. Of Mr. Chamberlain's House of i Commons otatement on the future of South Africa t is possible to speak in terms of qualified praise. He referred to the Boers with generous sympathy,apd obviously he is in earnest in his policy of concilia- tion. The mean plan of paying for the iconcentra- ton camps, half-rations, coffins, and all by , confiscating burgher farms has been abandoned, We may soon hope to see some tentative beginnings ot self-government. A risk lies in the evident intention to employ "National Scouts "—Boer deserters who joined our flag—as policemen. We could hardly choose agents more likely to excite the contempt of their countrymen. Lord Miler's conspiracy against the Cape C3nstitution has been defeated for the time being; but, as Sir illiam Harcourt argued, we are making a grave mistake by employinghim further. He has shown his hand too openly. His sole idea of conciliation is to divide the country into so-called loyalists and disloyalists—a name which means nothing more than the opponents of his personal policy. The man Who sought to establish a regime of absolutism is ot the ideal instrument for the building up of a iroe self-governing commonwealth.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RETURN OF ACTIVE SERVICE VOLUNTEER.

Four Runcorn Volunteers who went out on active service to South Africa in the beginning of the year returned home on Saturday. Their names are Privates T P ilort, I'T Dale, S Wnitby and J Royle. They went out With the ,Ird Service Company in February and joined the battalion at Beaters Dorp Sergeant Platt, who went with them, is staying in South Africa. The steamship German which brought the Volunteers to England arrived at Southampton on Thursday evening. The men proceeded to Chester on Friday morning, had an ofll3ial recep- tion at the Castle, and were presented with their medals and bounties. Runcorn bas had representa- tives in the Imperial Yeomany and they are expected home shortly. ASSAULT ON A WIDNES CONSTABLE.

At the Widnes Police Court on Monday morning, Timothy Cummings. 3, Albion-street, Widnes, was charged with being drunk and disorderly, and also with assaulting Fo !ice-constable Thomas G reen wood on Saturday night in the execution of his duty.— The!constable stated that be found the prisoner very drunk, shouting and swearing, and had to lock him up. On the way to the police station prisoner be- came very violent, and kicked wirnms and struck him over the head with his fist. Witness called upon Police-constable Bretherton for assistanca,and with great difficulty they got the man to the station.— Prisoner said he wai too drunk and did not remem- ber,—Police- constable Bretherton gave substantially corroborative evidence.—Prisoner said he would sign the pledge.—Alderman Neil said it was a very great pity that he had nob done so before.—Super- intendent Keighley stated that the prisoner had been up eleven times before—four times being during the present year. In 1899 he was fined for assaulting the police.—Prisoner was fined 108 and costs for being drunk, and received a like sentence for assaulting the constable. • DEATH OF A FORMER RUNCORN RESIDENT.

The death took place on Thursday of last week at the age of 59 of Mr Mark James,of Appleton. Up to 12 years ago the deceased lived in Runcorn and was well-known and respected. He was then en- gaged at the Smelting Works of Messrs Ke'shall and Company as a silver refiner. After leaving Run- corn he occupied a similar position at the Copper Works of Messrs J 11 Dennis and Company, Widnes, where be was held in very high esteem by the officials and employes. For over twelve months he suffered from bronchitis and asthma, and this was the cause of his death. The interment took place at the Runcorn Cemetery on Sunday, the Rev H Morrison 'officiating. In the first coach were Messrs William, Mark, Tono,and Fred James (sons), and in the second coach Messrs John Ham (Liverpool) and Daniel B sbrown(brothers-in-law), and Mr Edward Merryfield. The bearers were Messrs W Hornby, JArnes ' Young, Edward Mason and George Dean. Wr ethos were sent by the following relatives: The widow and sons, Willie and Lizzie, Mark and Sally, Mr John Hain and family, and Mr Daniel Bisbrown and family. SERIOUS FIRE ON THE SHIP CANAL AT RUNCORN.

The steamship Seal of Glasgow, belonging to Messrs G and J Burns, of Glasgow, left Manchester at e.ght o'clock on Thursday night. On arrival at Latchford about eleven o'clock the chief engineer, Alexander Logan, discovered that a fire had broken out in the after-hold, which woe stored with general cargo, cotton goods, &I. Thinking that Runcorn possessed more facilities for dealing with the fire he proceeded on his way and arrived about midnight. An alarm was given and the fire brigade under Superintendent Wright, were quickly on the spot. Six lengths of hose were used and tha hold was deluged with water. The brigade continued at work until seven o'clock on Friday. morning, by which time so much water had been poured into the hold that the boat settled down stern first. The The order of procedure was then reserved, and the water pumped out of the boat. Very little damage was done to the boat, but the cargo was of course either destroyed by fire or ruined by water. Valuable assistance was rendered during the pro- gress of the fire by Superintendent Ennion,Sergeant Cooper and P.C.'s Bowyer and Davies, INQUEST/I AT RUNCORN.

On Tuesday, before Mr Dobson, coroner, at the Runcorn Police Court ,en inquest was held to aching the death of the infant male child of Charles Knott, of Back Gilbert-street. Toe medical evidence show• ed that the child had died from a f osion of the heart in consequence of the stomach distending by the child being fed. A verdict in accordance with this statement was returned.— At the same time and place an inquest was held touching the death of George Cox (47), 30, Trinity- street, labourer. The evidence showed that deceased had lived with his sister, Harriett Worrell, for 14 years, and had always had good health. He came home on Saturday and complained of pains in the stomach. He had some boiled milk and went to bed, but was up several times during the night. On Sunday morning be again complained and was given some tea and whisky. He then lay down on the sofa and Dr. McLennan was sent for, but before he arrived the man had died.—Dr, McLennan deposed that the man died from heart failure caused by excessive diarrhosa.—A verdict in accordance with the medical evidence was returned. FUNERAL OF A VOLUNTEER VETERAN.

The remains of the late Mr John Buckley, a veteran member of the local Volunteers, whose death took place on the previous Wednesday, were interred in the Runcorn Parish Churchyard on Saturday afternoon. Deceased was connected with the Volunteer', movement for 37 years and was the recipient of a hug service medal. He was 64 years of age. Practically the whole of his life was spent in the employment of the Bridgewater Canal Com- pany, and for 26 years he was foreman porter at the Top Locks Warehouse. He had been a member of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows, No. 382 for 40 years. Toe chief mourners present were : Messrs Edward and John Buckley (sons), Mr F Nickson, of Manchester, and Mr Radley (sons-in- law), Messrs D and A Shaw (brothers-in-law), and Messrs Martley and Lea. There were also present about 20 members of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows, several representatives of the Bridge- water undertaking and Mr Norman. The service was conducted by the Rev IV Li cholas M.A. The bearers were Messrs Bowyer, Pendlebury, Owen and Gleave. The coffin was covered with beauti- ful floral tributes sent by relatives and friends.

- . INDIAN GUESTS AT RUNCORN.

A party of Indian notabilities, who came as the 11 King's guests to the Coronation,paid a visit to Run- corn on Monday. They were en voyage from Liver- pool to Manchester on the Ship Canal on the s s, Charles Galloway and made a stay of two hours at Runcorn. They were receive I by Captain Heasley marine superintendent, and conducted to Bridge- water House where luncheon was served. Tne names of the visitors are the Hoc Na wab Hum- tazucldaula Mahomed Faivoz All Khan of Pahasu, representative of the United Provinces of India ; the Hon Nawab Pateh Ali Khan Kizilbash of Lahore!represeating the Punjab; the Hon Meherban G H. Vinchurkar, representing the Bombay Presid- ency; the Hon G H Chitnavis C.l.B.,representing the Central Provinces ;Rs,i J Barna,representing Assam; Maung On Ghain, representing Burma, the Hon John Pollen,L.L.D.,T.C.S.,of Bombay, political officer bi charge. After luncheon several speeches were made Captain Heasley extended a cordial welcome to the distinguished party and expressed the hope thatsthey would return to India favourably im- pros ed by the British Isles. Mr Barns who spoke ng ish fluently, said to an interviewer " We have ' been very much impressed by our tour. Our ex- pectations have been more than realised. The most wonderful of all the things we have been privileged to see has been reserved to the last and that is the Ship Canal. Such a marvel of engineering skill was really beyond our comprehension." After luncheon the party made an inspection of the Bridgewater Locks, which created great interest and then re-embarked for Manchester.

RUNCORN NOTES.

LBY A. CORRESPONDENT]. The Co-operative Stores annual meet.ing was rather a protracted oae and the meeting discussed some very important proposals. It is very probable that, are long, the would•be committee man will have to fight a harder contest than he does at present-, in order to gain a seat on the board of management There can be no question but there is something radically wrong with the present system of repro sentation. Under existing conditions a candidate has to fight a whole district, and the scale, tor his acceptance or rejection, is turned by the persoas in outlying districts who know little or nothing about him. The more business-like method would:be t 3 split the district up into wards and choose a man for each.

There can be but little doubt that the private traders in Runcorn look upon the Stores with a cer- tain amount of mistrust if not tdownright jealousy. This is due to thefact that there is really no telling at what point the society will eventually stop in its career of enterprise. The majority of tradesmen live in mortal terror lest the company encroach still further upon what were at one time the " pro- serves " of a privileged class. It can safely be asserted that there would be joy in the camp of the Philistines if the enormous turnover of the Stores could be diverted from its now customary channels into the shopkeepers' pnkets.Meanwhile residents. in Common with other towns, are watching the struggle at t. Cleans with eager ex2ectation.

Mr. Evans, the pioneer of the Runcorn society, will sever his connection with the system he has so strenuously upheld at the end of the present half year. His resignation will be the snapping of another link between the old-fashioned Runcorn or the past and the modern Runcorn of to-day. In the early days of the movement Mr. Evans reso- lutely applied his energies to the building up of the system which, it is contended, has done so much for the working-class, fie was early electe treasurer and maintained that position worthily to the pre sent moment. Co-operator and non-c..'o-opsrater alike will feel genuinely sorry that Ir. Brans ie suffering from continual

Halton ere long will be commit/-ed to a sew ,rage scheme which will cost the village a sum of at least £3,000 There can be no question but from a t•eta' cal standpoint that the brook is polluted even though the inhabitants do not suffer any deleteri- ous effects from the drinking of brook-water. There is absolutely no way of escape from the Leen, Government Board for the inhabitants. They wit; be compelled to embark upon the scheme no matte, how distasteful it may be to the taxpayer in general and the Council in particular.

The Council are to be complimented on the way they despatch public business. The meeting yes- terday lasted only eight minutes which near') equalled the performance under Mr Lightburn who holds the record with seven minutes. There was one important subject introduced,namely the neces- sity for students to obtain an elementary grounding in mathematics before grappling with the intrica- cies of science. Students who wish to ensure success will do well to ,q,ive a litte more attentior to the elementary portion of their work than thel have hitherto accustomed themselves to do.

The regatta in connection with the boating club held on Bank Froliday was a splendid success, sac the secretary, Mx Hulps, deserves hearty cong rate lation on the manner in W hch he pioneered the venture to a succe,sful issue. It is pleasing to record that the regatta is a financial success no lea than it was a spectacular one. Mr Mintage,' carried off in triumph the premier honours of tht day, and the handsome cup and camera, which h, won last year, now become his own personal pro party. Runcornians will eagerly look forward tc the meeting next year, and there can be no doubt but that the fete will become one of the town't hardiest and most popular annuals.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY AUGUST B. 1902 WARRINGTON SHOW. Yesterday's Proceedings at Walton Park.

Yesterday, Walton Park was once more the scene lof the annual show in connection with the War- rington and District Agricultural Society. It will I be remembere i that the society commenced thirteen years ago under the auspices of the Warrington Shire Horse Society. Success attended the annual mare and foal shows,and ultimately the Warrington and District Agricultural Society was evolved. Additional classes were added, and the show now comprises classes for shires and draught geldings, hunters, hackneys and harness,jumpers, cattle,pigs• batter, cheese, eggs, dressed poultry, and potatoes For a successful 'show—especially in a financia sense—fine weather is essential. During the past three years, however, the society has not been favoured with this necessity. Oa Wednesday when exhibitors were preparing to despatch their ex- hibits, rain fell almost continuously, and yesterday morning opened with weather ot a threatening character. Towards noon rain fell heavily and it looked as though the show was doomed to experience another wet day. In the after- noon, however, the weather took a more favourable turn and continued fine to the close of the show to the delight and benefit of all con- cerned. The number of exhibits this year compares most faveurably with last year's total. The cata- logue showed 42,4 entries as compared with 394 last ' year. Thera were more shire entries and consider- ably more draught geldings, this latter class being a special feature of the show. There was a re- markably fine exhibition, in fact one could not dud better quality anywhere in the coun- try. All •the classes were well filled and there was the keenest competition throughout. In several instances the services of a referee were required. The defeat of the London winner "Red- I lauds Duke " by an animal belonging to Mr Edward Davies. of Partington, created great surprise. "Redlands Duke "carried off the first prize at Ad- lington on Wednesday. He has won prizes all over the country, a notable one being the Coronation Cup at Liverpool. Mr Davies's animal is, however, a weighty specimen of great promise. Some fine animals were also exhibited by Colonel R I Black- borne, Mr James Gould, and Mr E Gibbon, the former gentleman's exhibits obtaining the principal awards.

In the district clases for hunters there was a substantial improvement on previous years, and the hope was generally expressed that the farmers in the neighbourhood would see their way to breed more high-class horses of the hunter clav, owing to the big demand for such animals. The open classes contained some of the best examples that could be found in the kingdom and the exhibits of Mr 3 H Stokes, of Market Harboro', were a lung way superior to any other exhibit in these particu- lar classes. One of his animals, " Tennis ball," has won prizes all over the kingdom. It would have been interesting t 3 see Mr Stokes' " Tennis Ball " and "May Day "competing in the same class. The hackney classes were also very creditable: The cattle class: s were uniformly good, and, if any- thing, they were better than last year. An iimprove- 'neat was especially noticeable in the clasees Ur dairy , cows. In class 35 Mr Richard Clarke, of Warburtou, obtained the first prize with an excellent beset which was succtsgul all over the country last year. Mr John Longshaw. of Grappf,nhall, was awarded the first wiz?. for an animal of first Clips quality. So equal did tee judges consider the merits of these two dairy cows that, in awarding the special prize, they were compelled to call in an umpire, who decided in favour of Mr Clarke' s exhibi!.. In one of the classes I for bulls Mr Clarke carried off anothe:, firvt and a first was also awarded respectively to Mr W Gould, of Lymm, and Mr James Parr, of Hatton, all I three animals being very favourably comm(ntecl upon I The heifer exhibits were good, and in ens of the classes, Mr G J Baxter, of Stretton, won the first and third prize?.

For the first time three classes for pigs have beeo added to the list of entries, but the number of competi- tors was few owing to the fact that the district did not know of the addition, and also to the stringent police regulations with reference to the removal of swine. I, There was a distinct falling off in the entries for , eggs, dressed poultry and potatoes, the only apparent cause being the unfavourable season during the early part of the year. There were some excellent exhibits of Cheshire cheese, especially in the smaller size, which ranged from 20 to 30 lbs. The butter exhibited was of nice quality. The secood priz 3 should have fallen to Sir Richard Brooke, of Norton Priory, but as the cot ddions respecting weight were tot complied with, the exhibit was dhquaiified. Some very good specimens of eggs were shown, particularly ire the coloured variety. There were very few entries in the potato claws, but some nice samples were shown. Another special feature of the show was the divisicn of 19cal tradesmen's tarnou.:9, there being three classos instead of two as in former years, thus enabling the light aucl heavy horses to be judged separately. There were eight stands in the show grounds for advertising purposes, the exhibitors including Garton's, Limited, Seed Growers and Merchants, Warrington; Dutton and Bean. Agricultural Imple- ment Ag-nt4, Warrington ; William Cartwright, Honey and Bee Appliances, Moore,near Warrington. Mrs Massey, Acton Grange Nurseries, near War- rington,and J A Morrisma,Seedsman,Golden-square, Warrington. During the afternoon the band of the Liverpool Con- stabulary played an exceffent selection of music.

A -CO-OPERA-TIVi CONVALESCENT, HOME.

The North Western section of the:Co-operative Union called thqattention of the society to a move- ment directed to the establishments of Convales- cent homes for co-operators. A home worthy of 1-,uch a movement could be erected for £25,000, and the committee suggested that the money be raised iby shares and that each society becoming& member of the Convalescent Homes Association should take up over £1 share for every forty of its members and should subscribe such sums annually as it may agree upon as a maintenance fund, and for every .C 1 subscribed a society should be entitled to a recommend not to exceed two weeks' residence in the Home for any convalescent person it may nominate.

Mr Dudley said that the Scotchmen had hitherto been to the front in establishing these convalescent homes. The Cu-operative Wholesale Society had established one at Rowdon, 'Shrewsbury. The homehad been established out of the profits of each half-year. The North-Western Society had now taken the matter up and proposed to erect one at Norbreck. It was time that the co-operators should awaken to the importance of the matter. Toe Runcorn society would have to pay £1 per 40 mem- bers which would be about £l4O. That would entitle them to send a member for tyvo weeks for .£1 to the home. The secretary said that the feeling of the com- mittee was that the Runcorn society should sup- port it. After further :discussion it was resolved to con tribute .£l5O as share capital and to subscribe a• further amount per annum to be decided on when the money was required, on the motion of Mr R Gibbs.

RESIGNATION OF AN OLD OFFICIAL.

The following letter was read from Mr J Evans, treasurer of the society :—" In consequence of my advanced age and ill health. I am obliged to resign my post as treasurer to the society with the close of the present half year." The Chairman said all would regret the step Mr Evans had felt compelled to take. He was an old and faithful and valued servant of the Society.

Mr Lloyd thought none had done more for the society than Mr Evans, He was a member of the society in its infancy and he was not afraid to shoulder a heavy load across to Widnes. Practically speaking he was one of the pioneers of the society. He hoped when the tim came that he would receive a suitable testimonial to show their appre- ciation of the services he had rendered in the past. (Applause.) I.lr Wittington thought it would be quite feasible far the committee to consider the pensioning off of ' such an old servant. (Applause). The Chairman : We shall be allowel to discuss this matter at a special meeting.

"MUNICIPAL TRADING."

lhe Salford Electricity Inquiry. _ Judge Parry's Decision.

His Honour Judge Parry has just issued his report upon the evidence which he heard some time ago in connection with certain ellegatoos which were made in regard to the electricity undertaking of the borough of Salford. The matters which be was asked to investigate were tr--(1) As to the building, .5.;; of the Walness works; (2) as to whether the aforesaid works could have been enlarged (so as to use the machinery at present con- tained therein) sufficiently to supply electric current for , the necessary fighting and to run the tramways at the 1 present time and for the next five years ; (3) as to the , total power provided in the Walness works and the new works, and if found to be in exceas of the reqairements to go Gismo sghly into the matter 14nd report to the Council as to who is ri sponsible ; (4) as to all matters appertaining to any specifications, tenders, or contracts in connection with the erection of Strawberry-road Station ; and (5) as to any other matters that should be inquired into.

;Mich regara to the first question, his Elooour has come ta the conclusion that the " Committee could not have done more or other than they did. they worked bard to obtain information and acted on the best advice they could obtain, and the site they chose for the works was chosen after careful consideratioa and upsa expert advice."

Oa the second question Judge Parry has come to the conclusion that, from what he saw at the new works,acd for the reasons given by the witnesses on the subject; he ,1 is clear that the Corporation exercised a wise forethought in movieg the works to the new site, and that they would have incurred a very large expenditure in trying to make the Maness-read site suitable, without any probability of ultimate success. Given a proper foun- dation and agreeing to pull do en the present Wetness- road betiding, or to raise the roof to allow the new engines to be built, and agreeing also to spend the neces- sary money on the extra mileage of cable and the extra cost of coal-carting, a building probably sufficient for the next few years might have been erected, he says, but it would have been a costly and difficult matter, and not one that any responsible adviser of the Corpora- tion seems to have proposed. Ia his view it would have been a short-sighted and fcolish thing to do, and though it might have sufficed for a few years it would not have been providing tor the probable future needs of Salford. Discussing the question whether the total pc wer pro- vided was In excess of the requirements, and, if so, who was responsible, his Honour states teat there was at one time a kind of suggestion made that some parties bad pressed forward and advocated large and excessive works in order that they might get large contracts in connection therewith. Such a suspicion, he says, was , absolutely groundless, and after a long and patient in- quiry, and a careful examination ot all records and documents he desired to see, he could state, without the least doubt or hesitation, that if the total power pro- vided was excessive, that exam bad in no way been brought about by any fraudulent or wicked means, but had come about by a bona-fide though maybe mistaken notion of the needs of the borough. In this connection he states that he can see nothing blameworthy in the action of Mr Turner, the late electrical engineer for Sal- ford. The result of the committee's actions had been to give Salford an electrical supply in excess of immediate needs, but if he was asked whether it would be in excess of future needs he should refuse to prophesy. That the works were a credit to the borous,rb, and the result of an honest effort on the part of the Committee and their servants to produce what was supposed to be requisite, he had no doubt. At present there was an excess of power, but if they applied the historical analogies of gas,water,railroad,and similar undertakings it would be within probability that Mr Clirehugh was right when he spoke of the works as an intelligent anticipation of public demands. With relation to the eegine cantract,Julge Perry says he cannot See that anything wrong was done by Sir Richard Mottratn in regard to it. The boiler matter was even more remote from evil, aed he says he is per- fectly satisfied that the decision of both committee and Council was honestly arrivel at. and that no one sought to influence them in their decisi on by unworthy or im- proper means. %lett' regard to the allegations that the cable contract was not carried cut according to soecifi- cation,his Honour como'ains of the refusal ot Mr Whcat- pot a tion in al tupohprecisene r ttiic states matters.ampilne that i ai ndhoemnir down theiro d pointseconsideration does nby t tilir raisedakt; heb yt croft and Mr Turner to eive any particulars of their complaints, Be is of • opinion that no wrong has bees ne could done to the ratepayers by the deviations that were made and that Mr Clirettagh bad dealt honestly with the Col- Buc osliWueenfasut i theus rd t take t not trade l doing question g business with the Corporation, Judge Parry,after stating that that view is certainly not the law and is not a scosibls propositi la to put forward cone Mies that if that principle were sound either trade woul I be para- lysed or a municipal council must consist solely of those node .irables who had ne.cner tcade,oocunation,businass, nor profession, and therefore could not offend against Mr Hunt's principle. Puting the result of the law into what he calls " a few plain words," his Flouour says :—°' A ccu ncillor is not fettered ia carrying on his ordinary business in his ordinary way, or in trading with persons who trade with the Corporation. Be may sell whore he pleases, and that the person to whom he sells is a trader with his Corpora- tion is of no importance. Even if the things he sells , Come by ultimate resale into the hands of his Corpora- tion it does not disqualify him from his post. But be I must not enter into a contract with his Corporation, nor must he take a portion of another man's contract with his Corporationotor have any interest in such a contract He seems to no to be in it position of trust, and is put on rLas mthle icgoiry to dis- cover whether he is trading with nis Corporation through some other firm,for that is clearly forbidden. I need hardly add, perhaps, that anyone who seeks to evade the law by invoicing the goods in another's name, o" by any underhand means veks to trade with his Cor- poration,is far more likely to prove his disqualification than to escape the penalties of the seotion. An honest trader may by carelessness or mistake find himself trad- ing with his own Corporation, but there need be no reason,with ordinary care, why such it thing should take place. No amount of ingenuity, however, in the form and manner of sales can assist a dishonest man who tries to evade the law. If he passes his goods through half a deznn hands and his intoution is to trade with his a'rporatioo the complication of the transaction will not (Mist him."

WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB SPORTS. _- A Successful Meeting.

As usual, the athletic festival promoted by the Rugby Football Club was Warrington's chief Bank Holiday attraction. Despite the fact that some of the works were open, the attendance was exceed- ingly large, and the festival passed off with a suc- cess which must have been gratifying to those woo had been responsible for the arrangements. Fortu- nately, brilliantly fine weather prevailed, in tact the conditions could not have been, more favour- able. The programme was a long one—embracing 22 events—but the interest of the spectators was maintained to the finish. Some 'of the races were very keenly contested, the victorious competitors winning by very short margins. Credit is due to the following officials for the manner in which they discharged toe duties devolving upon them Clerk of the course, Mr T Bowers ; starter, Mr T Wainwright ; judges, Messrs J Evans, D Tinnion, J E Warren and T Locker ; referee, Mr H P Ellis ; judge of boxing competition, J Davies ; committee, Mes.srs W Nevins, W Hewitt, S Jeffrey, T Davies, J T Thorniley, F II eesoin, J T Bate, W Hill, I Hackett; handicapper, Mr H P Ellis ; hon treasurer, Mr T Pemberton ; hon secretary, Mr II Ashton ; 1r E Pickavance,N C.U.During the afternoon the Borough Brass Band played a number of selections.

ANOTHER ELECTRIC TRAM FATALITY AT ST HELENS.

At Parr, St Helene, on Bank Holiday, Mr Brighouse county coroner,held the inquest on Margaret Swift,aced two years, daughter of William Swift, collier, of 177, Chancery-lane,who was killed on Saturday afternoon by an electric ear in the roadway in front of her home.— The child's father said be saw the child in front of the car when it was about a yard away. He saw it relied ever by the lifeguard,and then one of the wheels go over it. The car pullEci up in abeua twenty yards. He could not Fey whether the train hell rang or not. Oliver Turner, the .driver, of 14, Hell-street, said he had been driving workmen's cars about twelve months, and an ordinary driver six • weeks.—The Coroner: Oh, thev make you practice on the workmen's cars, do they ? (Laughter.) The workmen will bs complaining.—Wit- ness said that on Saturday his car was fitted with a life- guard which is worked by the driver's foot. When he saw the child in fiont of the car he turned the brake and dropped the lifeguard,but failed to catch the deeeseed. The car was travelling about eight miles an hour. He thought twelve yards a fair estimate of the distance the car went without stopping,—The Coroner. Hew is it the lifeguard did not catch the child ? lent one or those Mr Cutter fixed 2—Mr Vaux : Yes ; it is approved by the Board of Trade.—The Coroner: Why don't sou get the new guard fitted 2—Mr Vaux : We have ten cars fi:ted, and we are putting on more as we get them.— The Coroner said one of the new tiod of guards might have saved the child. Ile recollected seeing the old guard try to pick up a dummy opposite the Tswn Hail, and it was not a complete succesP. Be was afraid if he had been struck in the way the dummy was he would have wanted a cofisn and not a lifeguard.—Ferdinand Musowsky, collier, said be saw the accident. The car was travelling at the ordinary speed, and the driver did all be could to stop.—The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," but suggested that the oars should be fitted with the new guard, and also that they should not travel so quickly down the slope where the acci- dent laappenel. They did not blame the driver.

- DAMP SEAT QUESTION. Heavy Damages against Leeds Corporation.

- • At the Leeds Assizes, on Saturday, Mr Justice Grant- ham and a special jury continued their hearing of the action in which John Hughes, a Leeds barman, sued the Corporation of Lae& fur damages for personal injuries, Plaintiff's ease was that on the 14th of August last he was riding on the top cf a Leeds tramcar, and, the seats being wet, was .tending, when the car, after going slowly for some distance, bounded forward with a jerk. Hughes overbalanced and was pitched into the road. He was seriously injured about the head, and it was expected for a.time that be would oot reeover. He was still suffering from tte effects of the accident. 11.5 Lordship observed that he had been noticing tbe trams that morning. Re had seen several passengers, owing to the rain, sitting on the rail, with, apparently. the knowledge of the conductor. If there bad been a sudden jerk two or three people might have been pitched over. The question occurred to him which is the most careless, sitting on the wet seat or standing. Mr Brown, surgeon, of Leeds, said that the plaintiff was actually taken home from the Infirmary at the re- quest of his friends so that he might die at borne. His recovery was remarkable. Witness thought that he might be able to follow his employment in a few months.

Mr. Mac'skie maintained for the defence that the Corporation were not guilty of negligence. People who stood up on the tops of the tramcars took the risks en- tailed. The Corporation were under no obligation to cover the seats to keep thcm dry. Counsel also sub- mitted that the plain, if had not taken sufficient pre- caution to guard against accident. The jury were aosent ft,r an hour and twenty-five miontre. ultimately finding a verdict for the plaintiff for £259

WIRRAL AND BIRKENHEAD AGRICUL-

TURAL SOCIETY.

The New Show Yard

This year the V—Virr.:l and Birkenhead Agriculural Society, which was incorporated and reconstituted at the commencement of the year, celebrates its diamond jubilee. A rite for new permanent show yard consist- ing of 22 acres, abutting on the Old Chester-road and almost adjoirung Babington Statior, was purchased from Mr Laver last year on tehalf of the society. Rapid pro- gress has ber n made with the immense undertaking in- volved by the preparations for this year's show, which will be held on the 10th and 11th of September. A fine , suite of entrance buildings, containing offices, tress room, members' rooms, etc., have been erected, ' and form a most imposing piece of architecture. I The approach roads to the main entrance have been sectored and curbed, the main road being 120 feat wide, and each branch road 30 feet wide. Permanent stables, have been built wherein the live stock sent for exhibition will be stalled,While permanently roofed sheds have be en built for the exhibition of implementr-, machinery in motion, 8,.:c!. The grand stand at the horse, Iring is a permanent brick erection, and will seat 2200, People. The ring, coli.cla is the largest in Eoglanc, is .enclosed from the other portion of the show yard, and only the society's members and subscribers will be admitted without yasment. The foundation Of the horse track is being constructed of ' heather, at d the entire ring is almost com- pleted. The convenience aud comfort of exhibitors and visitors will be admirably catered for by the whole plan of arrangements. Gas and water hare ben laid into 1 the yard,and already the Council have contracted to spend upwards of £15,000 independently of the amount paid for the land. The new show yard when comtleted will be beyond question the most concenlently arranged and the finest in the kingdom. Mr flannury,Minister ef Agriculture,has kindly promised to attend the show ,and. be present at the luncheon on the first day, and the pre- sident M.P,, will also e spirCles't el /i to! ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

Linen is the favourite material for useful frocks this summer, but the fact should be kept in mind that there are only a few varieties which make 'up successfully. These consist sof the coarse-grained French linen trimmed with Irish crochet point or guipure lace, or piped and tabbed with bands of piqué, numerous little gilt or fancy buttons giving a finish to the dress. Another more expensive variety is silk linen, which is one of this season's novelties. It is true, they are not cheap, but their loose surface and silky sheen make them infinitely preferable. The trimming of these toilettes is their own selvedges, which are usually somewhat fanciful, in black• and white, red and black, or mauve and green. Occasionally they are of a pretty tartan or plaid design, and are arranged in mitrings round the skirt, and form the tabs and pipings on the bodice. There is generally a selection possible in a whole gamut of shades of blue, from the palest to the darkest. Grey tones are also numerous, with an undertone of pink or green. The smart little coat depicted could be truth- fully termed a sac bolero. It is newer than the Eton coat or banded bolero and is arranged in box-pleats with a coarse lace collar. It is worn with a hat trimmed with Mous, and ends of bright emerald green silk, while the waistband

is of a corresponding. shade of green leather. These brightly polished leather belts with leather buckle in all shades are quite the latest novelty, and one which renders many an other- wise plain costume quite distingue. Bright green, or a pleasant orange tone of leather, is par- ticularly smart. There is another belt which is also new, and is made in the shape of a Swiss belt, both back and front ending in loops and cords at the side. This is made of coarse crochet twist.

White mohair of a coarse make, can be effectively turned in boating gowns, and can stand any amouot of hard wear. A dress of this Itind looks well piped with red and trimmed with coarse lace. Nothing really looks smarter as a trimming on white than the deep ecru grass lawn or yak laces, still lately a feeling has been evinced for embroidery of all sorts. The old-fashioned crewel work has again been revived, and offers an opportunity • for indus- tridus fingers to employ spare minutes and half-hours in the working of floral or fanciful designs in different colours, to be afterwards turned into collars or cuffs, or bandings of an ornamental character. " Teneriffe " embroidery harmonizes well with linen toilettes.

The pretty hat sketched is of fine straw bent into shape and trimmed with roses and lam

A SMART HAT.

Another charming style I saw lately evinced a more picturesque tendency, being trimmed solely by a scarf of lace draped on the brim, while a cluster of pale 'pink-shaded banksia roses lifted the hat up from the hair. It was secured to the head by long strings of pale pink gauze coming right across the crown and down on either side, where it was tied, the ends being left long. Very becoming to a young face are the hats of drawn tulle araped with lace, with large undu- lating brims, and in some instances these follow closety on the lines of the coal-scuttle bon•net of olden times. These hats are eminently becom- ing from a front view to a small-featured face, and, of course., go well with a short-waisted gown, but one cannot deny that, seen from the back, they are not without faults. There is, however, in the world of millinery quite a noticeable tendency towards modes whose charms are usually coaned to autumn. For instance, fruit and feathers are more worn by the ultra- fashionable than flowerp Yeddah straw is very much worn, especially by those women who are wearied of the rather übiquitous Panama. This charming dress is made of white crystalline, a silky, wiry material which does not crush or soil easily. It is trimmed with deep coloured Irish guipure, making a most effective contrast. The band and chou are of that particular tone of green termed rert amande. The wide hat is trimmed with creamy roses and

A CHARMING GOWN.

foliage. The bodice is plentifully tucked, and is made without a collar. Black and white has been much en evidenre this summer, and white muslins and zephyrs spotted with black make up into very chic gowns. There are some particularly charming clear muslins of this description, and these have, for the most part, a wide sash worn round the waist, falling in ends on the skirt behind. Everything in these dresses is studied for picturesque effect, and one very pretty bodice had a cross-over triple cape or fichu piped with white satin, the sleeves being gathered closely to the arm on the shoulders and falling in a wide bishop's sleeve to the wrist, where they are tied in with white satin ribbons. This is the shape which the newest sleeve takes, the gathers at the top of the arm giving a sloping appearance to the shoulders, which, of course, emphasises the leaning. fashion is taking towards the modes which. weresdashionable in the early days of the late . ;Queen. These muslin gowns should ha lined with muslin made full, as it gives the bodice a, softer effect And is cooler than silk,

COQUETTa.:

The King ham found the aid of a walking stick very useful during the latter stages of his convalescence, and though His Majesty has a large collection, his favourite stick is the one which was made from a branch of the oak into which Chs tees 11. climbed to escape Cromweler men, after the Battle of Worcester. There must be existence many ether relies of that celebrated oak, f( seen atter the Restoration, it was almost out away 1. visitors Who went to see it. The treeitself perished lone ago,and the present one is from an acorn of the original. During the many years our late Queen required the assistance of a staff, she frequently made use of the Boscobel walking stick,which is one of the most treasured heirlooms in the King's possessions. Some entertaining is taking place in honour of the Coronation, and the numerous Royalties, representing the Russian, Danish, Greek, Belgian, and Rcumanian Courts, who have returned in order to be present at that solemn celebration. Several distinguished hostesses in- cluding the Drchess of Devonshire, the Marchioners of Londonderry, and the Duchess of Portland, are fulfilling their original engagements, in respect of these Soc'oty gatherings, but of course the entertaining is not on the same grand scale as was contemplated in Juce,and July. Mans' people of rank and fashion who have been spend- icg the past two weeks at Goodwood and Cowes, are only making a short 'stay in London for the Coronation, and are leaving at once for the country or abroad,where they intend to spend the Autumn. There will be a large gathering of the Royal Family in the Highlands this autumn, the Duke and Duchess of Fife being amongst the earliest arrivals. The Duchess enjoys nothing so much as spending long days in fishing on Deeside, but the Duke of Fife is, like his Majesty the King, more fond of the gun than of the fishing rod. The Queen, ae everyone knows is devoted to angling, as is also the Prince of Wales, who is one at the best and keenest anglers in the Royal Family. His two sons, Prince Edward and Prince Albert are both keen on fish. log, and no mention of Royal anglers would be complete without including Princess Victoria and Princess Louise, Duchess of Argyll, who are both very expert with the ' rod. Rouge, Powder, and white washes, are not altogether 1 unknown in the feminine world, but the number of women who habitually make up" with artificial dressings is very much less than it used to be. It would be strange if it were not so, seeing the much healthier out-of-door life which the msjority of women now lead, as comea,ed with what was the general rule 20 or 30 years ago, There are neverthclees many women MO addicted to the use of washes and cosmetics that they consider it necessary to take them to the country cr the seaside. All such applications are more or less injurious, and even those who consider that they have no com. plexion to ruin, have to pay for their use in prematurn wrinkles and a disfigured countenance. Many vremen Rod girls who are constantly applying poisonous prepara- tions to their skin, might give Nature a chance, and Bee, espeoially whilst on their holidays what the fled] free country air, and a wholesome life would do for their natural complexion. The woman who would be popular should never recommend a dresersilker. An ill-cut sleeve, or a badly hung skirt can breed the most bitter resentment, and though it is the dressmaker upon whom the onus should fall, human justice is so appallingly uncertain that it not infrsquently happens to be the woman who recommends the modiste who has to suffer for that mediste's sins. Very much the same sort of punishment falls upon the individual who in an unwary moment recommends a "good holiday place." There have been cases and many of tbem in which firm friends have been driven into an enmity which has defied the softening influence cf time, merely because ore who loves solitude, and the beauties of nature, has been persuaded by the other to take his or her holiday at a jaunty seaside place,or lice versa. The choice of a holiday place is one of the things of which every man and woman should take the responsibility upon them- selves. For even if they altogether fail to enjoy them- selves and lose their tempers,they will thus at least kcep their friends.

Veils hava been little worn this summer, but during the holidays they are likely to he much patronised again. The newest veiling is very clear and light, and is arranged very full over tbe bat or tcque, from which it falls loosely to below the chin. Some women gather it here, slightly, thus forming a kind of bag, but the latest idea is to leave the veil to fall as it will. It seems early to talk of autumn fabrics, yet the new patterns are out, and soon we shall have to consider them seriously. It would appear that the day of rough and long-haired materia's was over, and that the plain smoother varieties would this season take their place. So far as the cut of our gowns is concerned, there is at present no sign of any change. A few leading modistes are building their skirts with the hip yoke, but this idea is not new, and moreover it is almost always a failure in actual wear. In the matter of sleeves, we should be ell advised not to tolerate anything in the way of exaggeration. As winter advances, Coey promise to grow smaller, and the " jelly bag" arrangement will probably shortly vanish altogether. The three-quarter sac coat, which has been familiar to us for the past ycae or two, is, it seems, to le worn again this autumn. This however, is a matter for re- gret as not one woman in a hundred looks her best in these lengthy and shapeless garments. The feminine figure is so obviously meaat to show:a dividing line at the waist, that any attempt to disregard it in one's dress is foredoomed to failure. It would be:well t ) bear this in mind, before making purchases, more especially es

EDUCATION. ---- The Bishop of Hereford's Eirenicon.

Mr Hirst Hollowell Protests. Mr J Hirst Hollowell, secretary of the Northern Cour- ties Education League, in a letter to the " Times " says :—The spirit in which the Bishop of Hereford has addressed you in regard to the educational grievances suffered by many citizens of England i 4 admirable ; and Nonconformists generally will recognize his desire Co be tree But he is seriously misinformed if he thinks both sides in'Lancashire will accept his proposals. The Arch- deacon of Manchester has no right to assare the Bishop of Heretord that Nonconformists will accept an arrange- ment whereby in 12,000 schools wholly maintained from rates and taxes, Nonconformists shall by statute he permanently excluded from the office of head teacher. This is the bishop's proposal in plain words ; and, it this is what se excellent a man pots forward as generous what is the policy ef the majority of the clergy ? Whit the clerical parts, wants is to subordinate the nation's work and the nation's teachers to ecclesiastical ascend- ency. This is what tee E location Bill does, and Bury, Leeds, and Clithere .say they will not have it. The whole country ought to be allowed to say what it thinks of such a bill. To pass it without a general election is to defraud the Country of its zonatitutemal rights and to destroy the most fruitful of its lc cal institutions. 'The bill is the visible form of a conspire cy against popular control and English Protestanism ; and it is the duty of every con- stituency to demand that, before it is passed into law, it shall be sent to the countr7 for consideration. The country would make short work of it. Miry ol the clergy know this, and are trying to hurry us into compromises. by which we should sell the schooe; into the hands of the S:ate Church for another generation. If there ever was so hour when Free Ceurctimcn and Liberals should stand fast that hour is now, The Rev. F. E. Powell and the Church's Education Policy. [TO TTiF EDITOR OF TIIE '• MANCHESTER GUARDIAN "] Sir,—When we see the terrible strife, " as when one letteth out water," which is raling nyee this Education Bill, we are surely constraiued to cry Seri boeo ? Any advantage which the Church is supposed to gam in one , direction is most assuredly more than lost in another. The Church's educational pellets has always seemed to I me feeble in the extreme. All eff drts to give definite religious teaching have been concentrated on Ceurch schools, regardless of the fact that these are slowly but surely being outnumbered by what we may now call State echoole. Supporters of denecninational schools may rest assured that this is the last kind arra degree of hclp they are ever likely to get from any Government. And in ten years' time how many of these schools will have been made over to the new edu- cational authority? There are many clerge, like myself, who realise that the game is not worth the candle, that the religious results are not anything like commensurate with the expectatione formed concernine them. And ' even if they were, this bill will make the struggle for I existence far keerier in many cases than it has ever been I before. There will be a falling off in those subscribers who have hitherto only given to prevent a school board rate being levied. Such subscribers cermet he regarded as friends of education, bet rather its enemies. And the lessened subscriptions . will in many instances be quite inadequate to meet tie dem +n Is which the new educational authority will make upon the managers. Toe days of deneminatinnal as distinct from State scheok are numbered. Far better would it hare been if our leaders had promoted a prlicy which by now week] have been successful—a universal State system, with the right for parents to obtain, at the expense of the venous reliedous hedles, such religious teaching as they (the parents) desire their children to have. Now we are in the absurd position of fiehting tenaciously for Church teachirre in one yet of schools, aed Church teaching, ton,f or all attending them whether Clinch children or not, who di not make use of the conscience clause, while making nn effort at all to secure Church teaching for Church do limn in the other and steadily increasing set of schools, but rather helping to formulate that " raoral mons- ter " State undenominationalism. But if our leaders acquiese in thia undeneminationalisrn for the million Jet us sac) Church chitdren in Board schnols, why should they be so tenscions for the other millio (let us say) Church children in Church 5C130,18 But while we are thus fighting a losing battle on be- half of our children only, we are incurring the hostility of a very considerable number of our fellow-citizens, whose cry that put,lic money must be entirely under public control is the only eound position for a self- reenacting State to adont. The Liberalism in me. which insists upon equal facilities being given for all religions, or none, to be taught in all Stats-aimported schools, will not allow me to use rhtes without the adequate control of the .ratepsysr. The Education Department has hitherto con'r ;lied the tease that have gone to pay for education in our Church schools, and if we are compelled to seek rate aid the only logical corol- lary is ratepaieers' control. I therefore am not surprised at the opposition which Mr Banc u,'.l measure is encnue- terire, nor do I think the Church is wise in seeking to maintain her infleerice in the schools by methods so alien to all principles of democratic government. After all, education is primarily a naticn I concern,and should be so regarded by the nation. And the nstion should no longer delegate its duty to religious bodies, particu- larly if by doing so these bodies are permitted to retain the chief and most nf the other posts for their I adherents. No one will pretend that Churchmen have a I monopoly of educational talent ; why, then, should the nation give them the monopoly of educe hone! offices in an overwhelming number of its ichools ? The Church, I know, is in the position ref landlord in these schools. Well, then, let the State bay rents for them or purchase them outright. Oh, but it is said, the east is So great. Until the uation learns that the cost of education is hue a secondary and subordinate consideration we shall never make that headway which for SO Many reasons we should. At any rate, the re- gent debates in the HOU9fi of Commons and the letterq in the" Guardian " have convinced me, what indeed I have felt for abog time, that, as a principle perfectly o Just and fair, Liberals are bound to go for popular con- trol, and I ouly wish we could place all schools in this position. proper provision being made for the teaching of religious principle 3 by aped at the expense of the various religious bodies to those children whose parente desire it for them. Then should we see a perfectly Jule and equitable arrangement and, what is scarcely second in my estimation, a termination Of the unhappy wife which is se terribly injurious to the spirit of true re- , ligion.—Yours, FRANCIS E. FONVFLL. St. James's Vicarage, warrineten, August 4th, 1902.

Warrington.

There is at present very little if anything in War ringon to indicate the near approach of such great event as the Coronation. 'the town wears ita usual'everyday appearance, no decorations having yet been commenced, and it is evident that the celebrations to-morrow will not be on an elaborate scale Of course, this is not surprising when it is considered how much expense many people in- curred before the postponement of the Coronation in June. A general holiday will be observed, the works and places of business closing for the week- end to-night. The Town's and FriendlY

Societies' Procession.

Complete Arrangements.

The principal feature of the cel-brationA at War- rington will be tl.e Town's and Friendly Societies' procession in the morning, the arrangements for which are exactly the same as were mace in June. The procession will fall in at the Town Hall

Grouurds in the following order, in formation of four deep, under the direction of the Town's Marshal (Mr Luke Talbot, Chief tion6tab:e)

1 Detachment of Police. 2 Volunteer Band and Battalion. 3 Ambulance Brigade. 4 Fire Brigade. 5 Clergymen of all denominations. 6 The Mayor, Mayoral Suite and Borough member. 7 Freemen of the Borougla 8 Aldermen, Councillors and Justices of the Borough. 9 Principal Corporation Officials. 10 Guardians of the Poor and Rural District Councillors. 11 Friendly Societies (in ,the order shown in the table published above.) For convenience of expression in this programme the above-mentioned bedies,from number 1 to num- ber 10, will be referred to as section 1 of the proces- sion. Number 11 (iriendly societies) will be called section number 2. At 10 a m the Marshal's bugler will sound the Assembly," acid section number lof the proces- sion should at once form four deep in the drives of the Town Hall, the Volunteer band and battalion taking up their position in the east drive with their band and head of column resting on the centre gate. The remainder of section 1 will fall in behind the battalion, in proper sequence, up the east drive and so on round the whole of the walk, the rear of section 1 being nearest to the west gate r Section number 2 will occupy the lawn., Entering the:Park by the centre gate (as per Time Table) the first Slciety (Ancient Order of Rechabites) will pass up the lawn to the side nearest Bath-street, and halt at the top facing the Town Hall.and the other societies , will form beside it in rotation. The waggons are to be drawn up in proper numerical order in Bath- street and Legh-street ready to enter the Town Hall grounds by Bath-street entrance and fall in behind their respective Societies when directed by the police. The first waggon should b 3 resting on the Bath-street entrance. No horse vehicles other than these (and such private carriages as may be authcrised by his Worship the Mayor), will be allowed into the Town Hall grounds, or to take part in the nrocession. At 10.30 when the Procession .should be ready to start, the bugler will a Jund the " Advance" The whole of the •processionista should then fees the Town Hall steps,where the Mayoress will wave the National Flag. At this signal the Volunteer Band will play one verse of the National Anthem, which the processionists are respectfully requested to sing in unison with the band, At the close of this cere- mony the procession will immediately move off upon its route, leaving the grounds by the centre gate. The Friendly societies will pass down the West drive and out at the centre gate.

Having traverNed the route all will return to the Town Hall grounds, section number 1 going in by the west gate and taking up its position in the drives exactly as before the start. Section number 2 will re-enter by the centre gate and take up the same position on the lawn as before the start. All will stand fast (in position as before the start) until a signal is ziverlby bugle-call, when all will face the TON7II Hall and a second verse of the National Anthem will be played and sung as before. The procession will then disperse. When the societies are re entering the grounds after traversing the route their waggons will go down Arplev-street and up Winmarleigh-street, et the top of which latter street No 1 waggon will stand, the others falling in be'And it. and all the vehicles will wait to join their reqpective sociel;ias after the dispersal of the Proce=sion.

Interment at Garswood. --- A Military Funeral.

A large crowd witnessed the funeral of the late Lord Gerard at Garswood on Monday, many journeying from netglabouring towns to do homage to toe dead peer of Garswood Hall. The baronial hall is situated within a mile or two of a busy mining population, and its scudded chimney stalks appeared to have held their blackened breath in favour of a day of mourning. All along the line of route tLere was evidence of the respect in which Lord Gerard was held by the populace. The doted shop of the trader, and the drawn blinds of the cottager, betokened the mourning, and signs indicative of grief were manifest on every hand. The close attachment his fate lordship had with the military—a career that was ever endeared to his heart— made it befiiting that he shouid have a soldier's burial. The interest which such a ceremonial must command was accompanied by genuine sorrow, not only amidst his lordship's retainers and tenantry, but amongst those wbo had no initnediate obligation other than the remem- brance of a good-hearted nobleman, who had always an interest in their lives, though separated by position and rank.

It was this sentiment that pervaded the multitude of hard-working Lancashire people who lined the thorough- fares and approaches to Garswood, where they assem- bled to pay their tribute of respect to the late peer's memory.

The tuneral took place at no rat at St o“vald's Church, Astiton-io-Makerfield, which is on the demesne of his late lordship. Long prior to the app iated time for the ceremony toe streets were lined with spectators, large crowds of excursionists journeying from Wigan and Sc Helens to witness the ceremony. At 10 30 the funeral cortege left Garswood lall, passing along the main walk of the estate,through woodland and emerging into Warrington load, where a large staff of police had been .301:11Mi6- sinned to keep the avenue approaching the church clear. The procession was particularly melancholy and im- prsasive. the body of the late baron being placed on a gun carriage of the Roy al Horse Artillery, which was attached to a khaki gun' drawn by four horses of the Artillery regiment, and followiog was the charger of his late lordship in trappings ( f mourning, 1?d by Corporal Moulding, who saw service with his lordship through the war. The coffin was enshrouded with the 'Union Jack. The deceased's sword, busby, and medal were also laid on the lid,along with a wreath from his Majcsty the King,

Immediately following came the main body of the Lancashire Hussars, who joined the procession with reversed arms and marched with a slow, funeral step. Colonel Elelowond. who was in command, was accompanied by Lords Lathom and Sefton, and other chief officerg. The band of the regiment, under Band- master Batley, played the " Dead March" in Saul on leaving the hall,and this was subsequently interchanged for Chopin' thrilling "Marche Funenr." In the mean- time Lady Gerard and her daughter, the Hon Ethel Gerard, enterAd the church by the side door leading from the estate. Her ladyship was dressed in black, and was accompanied by her immediate relatives. Thebody was met at the porch of the sacred edifice by the Roman Catholic Bishop of Liverpool (Dr White- side) and the following clergy : Deans Powell and Sommer, the Right Rev Monsignor Carr (vicar-general), Fathers Mverscoueh (Preston), O'Sullivan (Blaekbrook), Lawson, S.J., A Walmsloy (Liverpool), T Walmsley (Efaydock), W Parkinson (Orrell), E Smith (Pemberton), J Firzgerald (Liverpool), L Verbrugghe (Platt Bridge), J H Seed (Liverpool), L Barry (Widnes), J tdelling (Wrightington), Catterall (Liverpool), and Roberts (Ushaw)...

In the mean'ime the body was borne by Yeomanry to a catara'que in front of the altar, which was beautifully adorned with floral emblems. Following this the Bishop preceseled with the " Requiem Mass." The ctoir rendered the " Kyrie" and " Sanctus " to the Gregorian chant, and the " Jesu Salvator Mandl," and at the end or the Mass " Dona eis Requiem" to a harmonised Gregorian chant. At the end of the Mass the Bishop resumed his cope, and con- tinued with the Service of the Dead. The choir rendered in a flout-gluing manner the impressive hymn, " Dies Dee."

A Plant Breeding Establishment. Messrs Gartons of Newton and Warrington, have issued a very interesting and tastefully illustrated booklet in connection with their unique business o cross-fertilisation or "plant-breeding," AS mos people kpow, the experimental farm is situated a Newton, and the views in the booklet—which are all excellent examples of the photographer's art-- have been taken in and about this open-air establish- ment. Here we have presented in pictorial form some of the rich " stocks " Messrs Gartons have cultivated, tagether with illustrations of the various scientific operations in actual progress. The modus oAerandi, of the Garton system is really the marrying together, as it were, of any Variety of plant with another variety of the same species,and thus producing progeny or new breeds from the co- mingling of the plants. The experimenter claims to go even further than this upon the lines suc3ess- fully pursued by pioneer stock-breeders, and by what he terms composite breeding, to perfect any qualification in which the progeny was deficient. The principle of the crossing process is not new,but it is obvious from the results that no one has developed the same degree of proficiency in the engrossingly interesting but extremely intricate, and often disheartening work as Mr Garton has. As evidence of the tedious character of his Wk, it may be mentioned that it was only a few years ago , that the fruits of his. twenty years of arduous labours attained sufficiently defined results to justify him in bringing them before the public. RUNCORN AND WIDNES BRIDGE

COMPANY.

The fourth ordinary general meeting of the Widnes arid Runcorn Bridge Company was held at th offices of the Company, Liverpool, on Friday last. Mr li Seton-liarr M.P., presided in the unavoidable abie,ica of Sir John TBrunner Bart., M.P. The Chairman in meviog the adoption of the report and accounts apologised for Sir John Bivaneee absence, and stated that the accounts which were of a simple character, showed that only about 25 ptr cent, of the capital had so far bean expended. The report of the engineers, Means. Webster and Wood, of July 31st, wiich was read by the Chairman, stated that the work on both side' of the river had been proceeding eatisfactorly, ail the foundation cylinders on the Widnes side bad been securely bedded to the solid rock,and had been erected as high as the top making up length. These cylinders were fitted with cement concrete within a foot of the top. The bottom lengths of the columns for the Widnes approach girders were nearly all laid, and would soon be ready to receive the steel superstructure. On the Runcorn side the foundation cylinders were all in position and more than one half of nem had been bedded on the solid rock,and were now being filled with concrete. The excavation for the two anchorages on the Runcorn side had been com- pleted, the anchor plates and linki ware being placed in position,and .the concrete filling was proceeding. The excavation for the anchorage on the Widnes side had been commenced and was row progressing satisfactorily. The masonry and concrete work for the retaining walls of the Runcorn approach, the sub-way and the approach to the wharf ware well adVancEd, the main abutments being up to stringer Course level and filled in behind. The cab! as were in course of construc- tion at the St. klelena Cable Cnnpacy's works, and several satisfactory tots of the material and the sockets were made last week at the Birkenhead Chain and Anchor Testing Works. A large portion of steel for the main structure had been rolled at various works in England and Scotland ; all of which had been carefully inspected and tested in most cases with very satisfactory results, all rejected material having been replaced with other bars and plates which had satisfactorily stood the epeci- fled tests. The building up of this material to form the towers and the main stiffening girders would shortly commence, the contractors having assured them that the work would he ready to erect as soon as the lowest parts were ready to receive them.

The Chairman said he saw no reason why the bridge should not be finished within the expected time,and that as regarded calls on the shares these would be made as the progress of work required. The resolution was seconded by Mr H Wade Deacon, CA., and unanimously carried. A resolution WAS then adopted on the meti an of the Chairman, seconded by Mr H Sutton TiMMiS, J.P., giv- ing the company power to hold the half-yearly meeting in Jimuary, and July or Auguat in each year. Mr Handley, in seconding a vote of thanks to the chairman stated that a friend of his who had visited Rouen had expressed himself delighted with the TrAlLS- orter Bridge at that place.
