ICirappenhall,

At a meeting held in the school on Monday the ques- tion of fur:her celebrating the Coronation was discussed As previously stated in these columns, though the Coronation did not take place on the date first fixed, 26th June, the vtllage arrangements were carried out on that date and proved very successful, When accounts were settled a balance of nearly twenty pounds was left. At an earlier meeting it was decided, if there was a balance, to erect sorne lasting memorial of the Corona- tion. Different opinions were now expressed some being that another ctlebration should take place on Saturday, others that there should be a treat to the school children, while a third view was that the original idea ought to be adhered to. Finally the latter was agreed on and, beyond service in the church,the Corona- tion of King Edward VII, will not be marked by public rejoicings at Grappenhall. • Penketh.

BANK HOLIDAY.

The Railway Traffic from Warrington.

'the August Bank Holiday is always largely taken advantage of for holiday-oaakin,, and lae,t week-end there was the usto,.l exodus from WarrioVeno to popular 'resorts. By the London and North-Weztorn Railway Compant 's system ahout 600 peop!e travelled to Black- pool. Thera were 700 bookings to Frodsharn, Chester, North VVales, arid Ireland. A large number journeyed to Morecambe, the Like District, the Yorkshire Coast, West of England, the South Coast and London. The traffic, to Scotland Wail, as usual, heavy, Glasgow and E iinborough being the princioal places visited. Toe Ceestiire Lines Committee booked ! about 3.000 passengers to Liverpool and New ' Brighton, 1,500 to Manchester and Bite Vue, 1,000 to Southport and 300 to Boughs and North Wales during' the week-end. Wretched weather prevailed during the greater part of Saturday, rain falling very heavily. There was an improvement on Sunday afternoon, but in the evening there was again a heavy downpour. Con- trary to expectations, and to the delight of holiday makers, the conditions on Monday were almost all that could be desired. The sun shone brilliantly, hut at the same time the heat was not oppressive. The chief attraction for these who remained in town was the athletic festival promoted by the Rugby Football Club, and there was a very large gate. Tne electric cars were well patronized and in the evening there were large "houses "at the places of amusement.

MR. E. W. T. RICHMOND AS A PARLIAMENTARY CANDIDATE.

Central Hackney Divisionl

Dariug last week. Mr X W T Richmond has been very active in this Division, where he is the pros pective Liberal candidate, having addressed three ward meetings,

On Tuesday evening, an encouraging meeting was held in the Board School, Hackney Ward, when Mr R W Hall presided. Mr Richmond pointed out the absurd anomaly which obtains in the practice of olecting Progressive representatii.es on the London County Council, and then s,nding Unprogressive and Conservative representstives to Parliament to undo and oppose the work of the Council,emphasiz- ing the fact of the great betrayal of London by its Tory members. explained his views in regard to Home Rule, the temperance question and Regis- tration Reform. A vote of confidence was unani- mously and enthusiastically passed. On Wedne4day eveuing he addressed the electors of Hackney Downs Ward in the Bodney-road Con- gregational Schoolroom. Mr George Chambers presided. Mr Richmond expressed his gratification on the result of the Leeds Election. Referring to the BducatiOn question, he strongly condemned the attempt to place the education question of the country in the hands of tbe clerical party, and en- couraged the electo.s to demand a full control of the school.' for which 10-11ths of the cost were pro- vided out of public funds.A resolution of confidence was unanimously passed. On Thursday evening a very successful meeting was held in Ringsland Ward, Councillor John Sheehan in the chair. Dealing with the Housing and Temperance questions, Mr Richmond described them as the vital problems of our industrial life and the keystone of our social fabric. Referring to the bread tax and education, he asked whether the battles of our forefathers were to be fought over again, whether new corn laws must be repealed, and religious intolerance and Church rates again resisted. A scheme for Old Age Pensions was not impossible, and it would be to the bast interests of the nation to provide for the old, age of industrious workers. Mr Richmond has been most cordially received and unanimously approved, and the indications are everywhere apparent of a strong desire and determination to obtain a real represen- tative of the people for the constituency. The Liberal Association are organising a large garden party to be held in the grounds of Mr Garland on the 22nd of aeptember.

Buckley-street P.S.A.—Open Meeting. The monthly open meeting of the above Society was held in the Methodist Free Church on Sunday, when there was a large gathering of members and friends. Mr W Smith occupied the chair, and the speaker was Mr W Spruce. The lesson was read by Mr J Fairhurst. Mr 0 Gough was heard to advantage in his rendering of the solo q " Will you meet me at the Fountain? "and "The road to light," 89 was Miss Fairhurst in "0, rest in the Lord." Mr A Trow accompanied on the organ.

rHE SEWERAGE OF HALTON BROOK.

A Serious Difficulty

Halton Parochial Committee met on Tues,lay night under the presidency ot Mr Thomas Wright, and diacw,sel the qles4ia of the pohution Halton Brook.

Dr Adams submitted u special recort on the sub- ject pcioting ou tar. the brook vas polluted by the sewage from 3.1 houses p.n.l a tannery. He sug- gested that tilt sewage ah.;ul.l b t.eacea by mei.as of precipitation tanks and subsequent irrigation. A discussion ensued cluritui, which Dr Adams said if the committee applied for tanction for borrowing powers they would have to slic,w a scheme for the whole of the village whether it was eartisd into operation or not. 'lr Diggle, the surveyor to thts—lfur-alf:louncil,said the land stipulated for the purpose by the Local Government Board was one acre per 1,000 of the population. The presan- schema would require up- wards of two acres, dud the approximate c i.tt of he undertaking wonld be £l,OOO A schema pr - neec a few ye ago by him which was expecte I to involve £5OO was refused by the Local Govern- ' ment Board on the ground that the sewage wou'd ' run into the Ship Canal before being treated by pre- cipitation and irrigation. The Acting Clerk explained that they must show to the lfAcal Government Board a coronlete scheme to drain the portion of the village they intended dealing with. It was not eqsetarial to show' that they were sewering the entire village. Mr Barrow opposed the F.uggestion conteneing that the village was stationary and the rat,-able value so low that any trffort to meet the cost of the scheme would distress the poorer classes The Chairman replied that if the matter was delayed any longer the County Council won't' devise anlielaborate Scheme, and they would he compelled to meet the cost. They could borrow money for 30 years. It was decided to obtain a report upon the scheme from an engineer.

Major J Buekton. let Volunteer Battalion of the Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) has been granted the honorary rank of Lieutenant-Colonel. Major Bucliton joined the battalion as lieut. on April s:h 1882, and was ap- pointed Captain on April 25th, 1885. He received the rank of honorary major on May 26t1, 1897 and of major on July 4th, 1900.

A WARRINGTON DIVORL:E SUIT.

A Remarkable Case. Yesterday Albert Dyson Whitham, a wire-drawer residing at 35 Willis-street, Warrington, petitioned in the Divorce Court before Mr Justice Barnes for the dissolution of marriage on the grounds of his wife's misconduct with a man named James Monks. Mr Priestley (instructed:by Mr T S Steel,of War- rington) appeared for the petitioner and stated that the marriage took place on March the 10th, 1894, at St Clement's Church,Longsight,Sfanchester. Shortly after the marriage the parties resided in Dudley-street, Warrington. The wife soon began to neglect her household duties, and as a result of some words a deed of separation was effected petitionr paying his wife 128 a week. In Sep- tember 1895 petitioner took some steps to resume cohabitation, but failed. In July 1899 respondent took out a summons against her husband at Man- chester, where she was then residing, for desertion and maintenance. The husband at that time had just accepted a situation in Sheffield and as he did not want to risk losing his employment he did not defend the case. The consequence was that an order for 12s a week was made against him. In the following January respondent gave birth to a child. In February 1902, petitioner received an anony- mous letter, and as 'a consequence he and his solicitor, made enquiries in Manchester with the result that they found she was living with Monks the co-respondent at Bury-street, Gorton, and had had three children by him. The petitioner had since ascertained that during part of the time ha had been paying her alimony under the separation order made by the magistrates, his wife had beea living with Monks in Manchester, although he understood she was residing with her parents in York. This statement was borne out by the petitioner and Mrs Louisa Dixon who deposed to having attended Mrs Whitham on a confinement, and to baying seen her living with Monks as his wife. A decree nisi was granted.

SHIP CANAL COMPANY, HALF- YEARLY MEETING.

Warrington Traders and the Company. Important Case Pending. Mr. J. K. By thell, chairman, in meving the adoption of the report which appears on our seventh page, saki he regretted to have to tell them that a very serious and important case was now pending. Several firms at Warrington, on their own behalf and on behalf of al4 trrders at Warrington, together with the Corporation that town, had issued a writ. The Company's (1835 Act of Parliament laid upon them very serious obliga ' tions in regard to Warrington. Be supposed it was- a compromise effected by the promoters in view of the opposition of the Warrington Corporation and traders. The most serioue obligation was to dredge a portion of the river Mersey in the borough of IVarrington —not the Strip Canal, but the river after it left the Canal about Latchford and proceeded its course to the sea. It was also agreed to allow vessels; of 150 tons btu-then to nags over the Ship Canal between Warrington and Eaetharn at the low toll of only 4J per to when not trading outs'ile the Mersey. and vess-ls of eimil4r capsc- ity were allowed to make the same jourmy at 81 a toa if trading beyond the river Mersey if going to sea. In 1896, after considerable discussion on various points with Warrinvton,a new agreement was made and sched- uled in the 1896 Act. In that agreement provisions were made as 0 what should be done in the way of dredging, if they bad notice of obstacles in the river,and that the toll in respect of vessels trading not beyond the Mersey should be reduced from 4J to 31 per ton, anrs that they should be allowed to carry 250 tone instead of 150 tens. They also agreed that vesse/s trading outside the Mersey arid going to or neming from the sea sheuldi have the toll reduced from 8J to 61, and that there should be no limit as to the quantity of cargo they might carry.

A BIG CLAM. The statement of claim that followed 'the ism of the writ allzgrAi that they bad never carried out their dredging obligations, arid that while they failed Co do Una the traders, they claimed, might use the Carysl free of charge, and that thr,v were entitled to recover all charges opaid since 1896 with damagcs for nm per- formance of contract. Tne Company ncterly denied all these allegation.; and repodiated the claim. (Appltusr.) They had honestly ,nd vigorously carried out their ob- ligations, and they hoped they would be able to con- vince the court that they had done so. As an indioa- tion of that he would tell them that since 1895 they laNd expeadecl upwards o'f £45,000 in deepening the river, and that since then they had annually epect from five to six thousand pounds in the effort to main- tain the depth. That was a very considerable slice cut of toe sum nf £40,000 spent eae't ,ear no dredging. VIGORITS DEFENCE TO BE OFFERED. He was atire they would agree with the direetnra that an artina 'leo that had Co be vigorously defendcd to the very las% and it wouA I.e. (Apolause.)

ORIGIN OF THE "INTERVIEW.." , The clam that Mr W E Ftirster was the first public man ever interviewed in England draws an interaacing Letter from Mr E Legge in the tt Westminster Gazette." The method of procuring the views of public men wag practised =ay year before the date mentioned, MO. For erample, L-ord Chen Sir Garnett) Wolseley, on his return from the Ashanti campaign, good naturedly allowed the writer to " interview " him for the" Morning Poet," and on the following day Cllonel Fearing was equally considerate. The date was 1873 or 1874. Dia Carlos permitted Mr Legge to " interview " him in 1876 for the same journal. "To the best of my recollection," adds the corres- pondent," there was nothing p•rtieularly novel about the "interview "even in '73 or '74.

• THE LASH OF THE HECKLER. Mr Wagon has come under the lash of the heckler pretty freely in his tour among the Orcadians, and One or tw3 qaestious and answers may be quoted : Beater : As you advocate compulsory !Int purchase and the land for the people, will you vote in the house of Commons for the nst.onalisstion of the land ? Mr Was : I believe toe land is held bi the land- lords in trust for the people, and that if power were given for compulsory acquisition of land it would carry out the ancient law of the realm, which was that the State was really the owner of the land. HeAler : As you favour the nationalisation of the land, are you also favourable to the nationalisaticn of all tne capital of the csuntry ? Mr Wagon : No.

DARING ESCAPE bF DESPERATE CRIMINALS. The" Morning Leader's " Nt3W York correspondent r )rts that on Monday night sixteen desperate criminals marched out of the Penitentiary near Nastivillv. Jack The, an expert safe bowler, obtained dynamite from outside friends and blew a hole in the front wall. Gas Hyatt, a train robber of national repute, with a brace of revolvers held the guards at bay wnile fourteen of his and Doe's friends walked fob. Next morning Edward Carney, a famous confidence man, was killed by the Sheriff's posse. Two others were run down by bloodhounds and captured. Hyatt went to his sweetheart's home and took bet away with him.

WITHOUT A LICENCE, TOO I During the holidays in Manchester the Att Galleries have, as usual, been crowded with visitors, mostly country cousins from the rural districts of the cousty. The other dap an elderly couple stood gazing with admiratiou at the well-known picture, "Hawking in the Olden Time." " John," said the old lady, " what's that ? " *John tamed over the leaves of the catalogue. " They call it Hawking in the Olden Time,'" be said. aS" Hawking! -Wnat are they hawking ? " she in- quired. "I dunno." he replied ; "but I 'spect they're trying to sell them birds."

we are promised one or two new styles in coats later on. The newest things in fenders recalls the good cld nursery article, though it is not so high,and much more ornate and artistic. A very beautiful pattern carried out in copper, dm a centre in fine nettirg, with tall branches of lilies in the middle and on each side. A frieze of the lilies makes an effective top and the dad° upon which the fender rests, is in a very open flora! design. Crpper is coming more and more into favour for decorative purposes, frames, brackets, door plates, etc. ,all being turned out in this metal. Several parents have already named their daughters, born in the present summer " Corona " and probably there are others who contemplate doing the same thing Such parents should remember that in giving their daughters this name, they are, for the whole of their lives publishing their age to the world, which does not seem to be a very kind proceduie.
